Axions and Superfluidity in Weyl Semimetals
Emil Mottola, Andrey V. Sadofyev, Andreas Stergiou

Abstract
An effective field theory (EFT) for dynamical axions in Weyl semimetals (WSMs) is presented. A pseudoscalar axion excitation is predicted in WSMs at sufficiently low temperatures, independently of the strength of the Weyl fermion self-coupling. For strong fermion self-coupling the axion is the gapless Goldstone boson of chiral U ​ ( 1 ) ch U(1)^{\text{ch}} spontaneous symmetry breaking. For weak fermion self-coupling an axion is also generated at non-zero chiral density for Weyl nodes displaced in energy, as a gapless collective mode of correlated fermion pair excitations of the Fermi surface. This is an explicit example of the extension of Goldstone’s theorem to symmetry breaking by the axial anomaly itself. In both cases the axion is a chiral density wave or phason mode of the superfluid state of the WSM, and the Weyl fermions form a chiral condensate ⟨ ψ ¯ ​ ψ ⟩ \langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle at low temperatures. In the presence of an applied magnetic field the axion mode becomes gapped, in analogy to the Anderson–Higgs mechanism in a superconductor. ’t Hooft anomaly matching from ultraviolet to infrared scales is directly verified in the EFT approach. WSMs thus provide an interesting quantum system in which superfluid, non-Fermi liquid behavior, and a dynamical axion are predicted to follow directly from the axial anomaly in a consistent EFT that may be tested experimentally.



Contents

1
Introduction: QFT Axions and Emergent Axions in WSMs

Condensed matter systems can host excitations considered in relativistic quantum field theory (QFT)
and high-energy particle physics, but which have remained unobserved or difficult to study in that setting.
An interesting example spanning these subfields is the emergence of chiral fermion excitations in Weyl
semimetals (WSMs). In a WSM, electronic valence and conduction bands intersect the Fermi level at isolated
points, the Weyl nodes, close to which the quasi-particle spectrum is linear and gapless. The low-lying
excitations are thus Weyl fermions
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
, described by two-component
spinors with momentum and spin either aligned or anti-aligned, and hence states of either positive or negative
chirality respectively. The effective Hamiltonian of these gapless Weyl fermion quasi-particles is identical
to that proposed by H. Weyl almost a century ago
[
5
]
, but so far not observed in high energy
physics, with the Fermi velocity
v_{F}
of the WSM replacing the speed of light
c
in relativistic QFT.

According to the Nielsen–Ninomiya theorem
[
6
]
, such Weyl nodes must arise in pairs,
with each pair composed of opposite chirality Weyl fermions. Although a WSM often has more than two Weyl
nodes, the minimal and simplest situation is that of a single pair of Weyl nodes, which has also been
encountered
[
7
,
8
]
, and upon which we focus for simplicity in this paper.

The emergence of Weyl fermions in WSMs leads to a direct connection with the chiral (also referred to as the
axial, triangle, or Adler–Bell–Jackiw) anomaly
[
9
,
10
]
, the physical consequences of which can
be studied now in a setting very different than the relativistic QFT context in which it was originally found.
In WSMs the chiral anomaly has been shown to be responsible for the quantum anomalous Hall effect and the
chiral magnetic effect
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. Recent reviews of anomalies and related transport properties in WSMs may be
found in
[
18
,
19
,
20
]
.

Related to the presence of the axial anomaly is the suggested appearance of an axion in
WSMs
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
,
i.e.
a pseudoscalar ‘phason’ collective
mode that couples linearly to the topological density
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F_{\mu\nu}=2F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=8\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}

[
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
. In the Standard Model of
particle physics, the strong nuclear interactions are also described by a gauge theory, quantum chromodynamics
(QCD), based on the local non-abelian color group
SU(3)
. Soon after QCD was introduced it was recognized
that the QCD Lagrangian could contain the topological term
\theta\,\textrm{tr}(G_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{G}^{\mu\nu})
,
which is the non-abelian generalization of
\theta\hskip 0.5ptF_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
, with
G_{\mu\nu}
the

SU(3)
color gauge field strength of QCD, and
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
is an arbitrary phase angle. The problem
is that such a term violates the discrete symmetries of charge conjugation (C) and space reflection parity (P),
whereas the strong nuclear interactions respect these symmetries to a very high degree of accuracy. In fact,
experimental data lead to a bound on
\theta
,
\theta\lesssim 10^{-9}

[
25
]
, consistent with
\theta=0
,
whereas QCD itself provides no reason for
\theta
either to be identically zero, or fine tuned to the
required small enough non-zero value.

The axion was introduced as a new particle to solve this strong CP problem of QCD, by promoting the fixed
constant
\theta
angle to a local dynamical field, which could relax to zero or a very small value by its own
dynamics
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
]
. Although the initial
axion proposal was quickly ruled out by experiment, it survives in modified form with much weaker coupling
to known particles, as still the most often considered solution to the strong CP problem of QCD. In addition,
a weakly coupled axion of this kind is a candidate for the dark matter that apparently makes up 25% of the
total mass-energy in the universe
[
30
]
. This proposed weakly coupled cosmological axion
also has not been detected, after more than four decades of intensive and increasingly sensitive searches,
which continue up to the present
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
. Given these null
results to date, from a particle physics perspective, it is worth considering alternatives to a ‘fundamental’
axion field for a solution to the CP problem. Thus any example where an axion-like mode can be realized as a
collective or emergent phenomenon of quantum many-body physics is interesting, for the possible light it
could shed on the CP ‘naturalness’ problem of the Standard Model. For this, it is important to identify
the requirements and physical mechanism(s) by which an axion can emerge as a collective mode in a realistic
many-body system that can be studied in a laboratory environment, where the parameters of the system
can be varied and controlled. This is just what WSMs provide.

From a condensed matter perspective, a possible axion excitation in WSMs has been discussed primarily
through the introduction of four-fermion interactions
[
14
]
of the Nambu–Jona-Lasinio (NJL)
type
[
34
,
35
]
, which induce the spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB) of

U(1)^{\text{ch}}
chiral symmetry. The axion is then the Nambu–Goldstone boson generated by this
SSB of a global symmetry. As a result of this SSB, typically described by the introduction of a scalar
field order parameter developing a non-zero expectation value in its ground state, the Weyl fermions
acquire a mass gap. A preliminary claim of detection of an axionic mode in a WSM has been made
in
[
21
]
, although this awaits confirmation and study of the excitation spectrum
and its detailed properties.

At the same time the axial or chiral anomaly breaks
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry explicitly, violating the
Ward–Takahashi (WT) identities of
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
invariance. Thus it is not clear how this explicit
breaking of a global symmetry by the anomaly is to be reconciled with the usual formulation of Goldstone’s
theorem, where the symmetry is assumed to be exact at the microscopic level, and only the ground state of
the theory spontaneously breaks the symmetry. The usual statement of Goldstone’s theorem and massless
Goldstone boson depend upon the existence of a bosonic order parameter and degenerate states of the same
energy, related by the symmetry. Neither a scalar order parameter nor the requisite
U(1)^{\text{ch}}

symmetry are immediately apparent in a fermionic theory possessing an anomaly, which explicitly violates
this very symmetry.

Despite these apparent differences with the usually considered requirements of Goldstone’s theorem, it was
shown in a previous paper that a gapless pseudoscalar chiral density wave (CDW) in the anomalous fermion theory
follows from the axial anomaly itself
[
36
]
. The CDW may be understood as a collective excitation
of the Fermi surface and a result of fermion-antifermion (particle-hole) pairing, similar to Cooper pairing
in a superfluid or superconductor. The gapless collective mode is made manifest as a
1/k^{2}
massless pole of
the axial anomaly triangle amplitude
[
37
]
. Thus it appears that the Goldstone phenomenon applies
also the case of anomaly symmetry breaking (ASB), despite—or rather as a direct consequence of—the axial
anomaly itself. Clarifying this state of affairs and applying it to WSMs where the pseudoscalar CDW is an axion
is the first principal aim of this paper. We will show that the familiar description of SSB by a scalar order
parameter and proof of Goldstone’s theorem can be extended to the case of anomalous symmetry breaking (ASB).
Indeed both can be derived from the same UV complete QFT in different limits and, remarkably, both lead to
essentially the same low energy effective theory of a relativistic superfluid with an axion excitation.

An interesting related issue is that of decoupling of heavy (gapped) degrees of freedom from the low energy
spectrum, and the status of anomaly matching between the SSB phase, where the fermions become massive, and
the ASB phase, where they were assumed (at least at first) to remain massless. The expectation of ’t Hooft
anomaly matching
[
38
]
is that the form and magnitude of the chiral anomaly should be
renormalization group invariant, and hence the same across phases and across widely different distance scales,
even if the fermions develop a mass gap.

Naively a large fermion mass gap might lead one to expect that the fermions and the entire axial anomaly decouple
entirely at low energies or large distances, which would appear to violate ’t Hooft anomaly matching
across scales. In the Standard Model this anomaly matching from the short distance scales of perturbative QCD
to the low energy pion dynamics of the confined theory is critical to accounting for the experimentally verified
low energy decay rate
\pi^{0}\to 2\gamma
, in terms of the quantum numbers of the constituent colored
quarks
[
39
]
. Historically this success played an important role in the establishing of QCD as the
theory of the strong interactions. However, a proof of anomaly matching between the short distance, high energy
quark degrees of freedom of QCD and the long distance, low energy theory of mesons in which quarks are
confined and do not appear at all in the low energy spectrum of QCD is necessarily indirect in full
QCD
[
38
]
, because a complete understanding of the mechanism of quark confinement is still lacking.

In the effective field theory (EFT) motivated for application to WSMs of this paper, it turns out that anomaly
matching across energy scales and phases can be checked and verified directly by perturbative methods.
Consequently, this EFT provides a consistent framework from UV to IR in which the fundamental question of
decoupling—or its opposite, ’t Hooft anomaly matching—can be addressed systematically. Providing
an explicit example of derivation of low energy superfluid behavior emerging from an EFT, itself
obtained from a UV complete QFT, in which the axial anomaly remains intact is the second principal
goal of this paper.

In order to avoid potential misunderstandings across different sub-fields, let us
emphasize that the EFT approach for WSMs of this paper, which takes full advantage
of the emergent Lorentz symmetry (with Fermi velocity replacing
c
), and Weyl symmetry
(broken only by the axial anomaly) that WSMs enjoy at their Weyl points, can apply only for energies sufficiently close to such a Weyl node. These symmetries are clearly not exact, since the linear dispersion
E\propto\boldsymbol{p}
for fermionic excitations near the Weyl node(s) upon which our approach relies, are violated by the non-linear terms in the
fermion spectrum and Fermi arcs at higher energies of the height of the Weyl cones
\Lambda_{W}
, typically of the order of
100
meV or more
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
. For energies well below this
\Lambda_{W}

WSMs provide a laboratory
realization of effective Lorentz invariance where the effects of the axial anomaly of
relativistic QFT and an EFT based on these first principles can be studied systematically,
and the consequences of these approximate symmetries are expected to apply for energies
E\ll\Lambda_{W}
at a Weyl node. For simplicity
we focus in this paper on a single isolated Weyl node and neglect the effects of the
non-linear terms suppressed by
E/\Lambda_{W}
, and other effects due to impurities, etc.

Let us also remark at the outset that another potential source of confusion
is the (at least) three different notions of EFT in common use in the literature:

(
i
)

The full one-particle irreducible (
1PI
) quantum effective action
\Gamma[\varphi]

obtained by computing quantum corrections to a classical action
S_{\rm cl}[\phi]
, independently of scale;

(
ii
)

The low energy expansion of this non-local 1PI quantum effective action in inverse powers
of the heavy mass
M
of some quantum field(s) which are completely ‘integrated out,’ to obtain a local
(
Wilson
) effective action
\mathcal{S}_{\text{eff},M}
applicable to energy scales lower than
M
;

(
iii
)

The hydrodynamic or
Fluid
effective action
\mathcal{S}_{\rm Fluid}
defined only by
symmetries, conservation laws and the equation of state of the system in local thermodynamic equilibrium,
applicable only at macroscopic distance scales, with no reliance upon any specific microscopic theory.

In the case of
(i)
the generating function of connected correlation functions
W[J]
is first defined
by the functional integral

\exp\big\{iW[J]\big\}\equiv\int[{\cal D}\phi]\hskip 0.5pt\exp\big\{iS_{\text{cl}}[\phi]+i\hskip 0.5ptJ\circ\phi\big\}

(1.1)

over a set of quantum fields
\{\phi_{i}(x)\}
, governed by the classical action
S_{\text{cl}}[\phi]
in the presence
of the external sources
J_{i}(x)
. In (
1.1
) the shorthand notation
J\circ\phi
is employed to denote

\int d^{\hskip 0.5ptd}x\,J_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x)
in
d
spacetime dimensions. The 1PI quantum effective action
\Gamma[\varphi]

is then defined as the Legendre transform of
W[J]

[
40
,
41
]
,

\Gamma[\varphi]\equiv W[J_{\varphi}]-J_{\varphi}\circ\varphi\,,

(1.2)

evaluated at
J_{\varphi}(x)\equiv J[\varphi(x)]
obtained by solution of the implicit equation

\frac{\delta W[J]}{\delta J_{i}(x)}\bigg|_{J=J_{\varphi}}=\varphi_{i}(x)\,,

(1.3)

which must be inverted to find
J_{\varphi}(x)
and inserted into (
1.2
).

The 1PI effective action
\Gamma[\varphi]
so defined is the generating functional of proper vertices, and the
\{\varphi_{i}(x)\}
are the
background or mean fields of the background field method
[
42
]
. If the original
S_{\text{cl}}
is renormalizable
and UV complete, its 1PI effective action
\Gamma[\varphi]
and the equations of motion following from it apply at any scale without restriction.
However, in general
\Gamma[\varphi]
formally defined by (
1.2
) is a non-local functional of
\varphi
, that cannot be calculated
exactly. Restrictions then inevitably arise in the approximations necessary to calculate it. As a familiar example, the one-loop
approximation to
\Gamma[\varphi]
for the
\varphi_{i}
spacetime constants (not necessarily fundamental fields) are the order parameters of the
effective potential
V_{\rm eff}(\varphi)
at finite temperature or density that can be used to diagnose SSB and the restoration of
symmetry
[
43
]
, as well as to prove the Goldstone theorem
[
44
,
45
]
. The 1PI effective
potential is therefore closely related to the Landau theory of phase transitions, with parameters
fit to data in condensed matter applications rather than calculated from first principles of microphysics
[
46
]
.

If some of the quantum fields in (
1.1
) are massive with large mass gap
M
, one expects that their effects can be neglected
at low energies
E\ll M
, or equivalently distances much larger than
1/M
, as a consequence of
decoupling

[
47
]
.
In that case, such heavy fields may be ‘integrated out’ completely in (
1.1
), with no sources or mean fields specified
for them. This procedure is also usually difficult to carry out explicitly, especially when the ‘fundamental’ or UV complete
theory is itself unknown. In that case one relies on the symmetries of the low energy theory and scaling dimensions to expand
the effective action in a power series of local operators of the remaining light fields in ascending powers of
1/M
. A
familiar example of this EFT approach is Chiral Perturbation Theory
[
48
,
49
]
. Since the separation of
scales specified by
M
is closely related to the Wilsonian classification of infrared (IR) relevant and irrelevant operators
in statistical and condensed matter physics
[
50
,
51
,
52
]
, the EFT
(ii)
organized this way in terms
of a heavy mass scale is referred to here as the
Wilson
effective action
[
53
]
.

Finally, at the lowest energy scales when almost no information is available about the underlying microscopic theory or its
fundamental interactions, one can consider a hydrodynamic or
Fluid
EFT
(iii)
which is based only upon symmetries
and conservation laws, and a specified equilibrium equation of state of the system. Assuming local equilibrium on microscopic
scales (
i.e.
on order of the mean free path and smaller) is maintained, small deviations from the equilibrium relations
at the longest wavelength macroscopic scales such as sound waves can be studied
[
54
]
.

Generally, each of these three meanings of ‘effective field theory’ is different from the others and the
relationship between them is non-trivial. The remarkable fact following from explicit anomaly matching in
WSMs is that the chiral anomaly admits a consistent description from fundamental principles of QFT connecting
these three approaches, starting from a single renormalizable theory of Weyl fermions coupled to scalars and
electromagnetism in both the SSB and ASB cases, as we shall show, at least at zero temperature and in a
pure sample without defects.

The organization of this paper is as follows. We begin in the next section
2
with a review of the axial
or chiral anomaly of fermionic QED
4
, and the gapless collective boson excitation of
the Fermi surface it implies. In Sec.
3
we show how the effective action of the axial anomaly applied to
a WSM with Weyl nodes displaced in energy may be written in a local form in terms of a collective phason field and
recognized as describing a gapless relativistic superfluid chiral sound mode, which is a dynamical propagating axion.
In Sec.
4
, we show that this phason mode becomes gapped in the presence of a magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}
, and
in fact coincides with the massive Schwinger boson of QED
2
, when its propagation direction is aligned with
\boldsymbol{B}
.
In Sec.
5
we show how self-interactions of the Weyl fermion modes may be incorporated into the WSM model, by
means of the Nambu–Jona-Lasino model, and Hubbard–Stratonovich transformation, introducing a scalar field
\Phi
whose expectation
value for strong enough fermion self-coupling describes the spontaneous breaking of the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
chiral symmetry, and
gapping of the Weyl modes. In Sec.
6
we propose a UV renormalizable theory with both fermions and bosons
encompassing both the strong and weak fermion coupling regimes, thus allowing a consistent description of both SSB by the scalar order parameter field and ASB by the axial anomaly collective mode itself at non-zero chiral chemical potential. In
Sec.
7
, we study perturbations from equilibrium in this theory and show that the long wavelength
Goldstone sound mode of the scalar
\Phi
is the SSB phase satisfes exactly the same dynamical equation of motion as the
axion collective boson of the axial anomaly at finite chiral chemical potential, thus verifying that ASB via the axial
anomaly itself must also lead to a scalar chiral condensate of
\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle
and Goldstone mode.
In Sec.
8
the consistency of ’t Hooft anomaly matching between the massless Weyl fermion and SSB
cases where the Weyl fermions become gapped is shown explicitly in the EFT across these two cases.
In Sec.
9
it is shown that Goldstone’s theorem can be extended from the more familiar case of
SSB by an explicit scalar order parameter field to the ASB case through the axial anomaly itself at non-zero

\mu_{5}
, so that the gapless axion collective mode of the previous sections is also a Goldstone boson. Sec.
10
contains our summary and discussion of results, looking ahead to future work.

There are also three appendices of supplementary material. Appendix
Appendix A. The Chiral Anomaly in QED
4
and its IR Aspect
contains additional details of
the axial triangle anomaly in QED
4
, and particularly its infrared apsect, associated spectral sum rule, and
derivation by an unsubtracted Kramers–Kronig dispersion relation. Appendix
Appendix B. The Axial Anomaly for WSMs in a Constant Uniform Magnetic Field
gives the details
of the dimensional reduction of the axial anomaly from
d=4
to
d=2
dimensions for a constant, uniform magnetic
field background, and the connection to the Schwinger boson of QED
2
described in Sec.
4
.
Appendix
Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass
gives the details of the integrating out of massive fermions, which provides the
connection between the NJL model of Sec.
5
and the Wilson effective action
(ii)
of the SSB
phase of a WSM where the Weyl fermion modes acquire a mass gap.

2
The Axial Anomaly in QED
4

The Lagrangian of Dirac fermions
\psi
coupled to electromagnetism with charge strength
e
is

\mathscr{L}_{f}=\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pt\gamma^{\mu}\big(i\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}+eA_{\mu}+b_{\mu}\gamma^{5}\big)\psi-m\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pt\psi\,,

(2.1)

where
\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}(\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\rightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}-\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}),{\bar{\psi}}=\psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
, and we have allowed for a non-zero fermion mass gap

m
and additional coupling to an external axial vector potential
b_{\mu}\equiv A^{5}_{\mu}
with unit strength.

The Lagrangian (
2.1
) is invariant under the local
U(1)^{\rm EM}
phase transformation

\psi\to e^{i\alpha}\psi\,,\qquad\bar{\psi}\to\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pte^{-i\alpha}\,,\qquad eA_{\mu}\to eA_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\alpha

(2.2)

for any
m
. When
m=0
(
2.1
) is also invariant–at the classical level–under the additional

U(1)^{\rm ch}
local chiral phase transformation

\psi\to e^{i\beta\gamma^{5}}\psi\,,\qquad\bar{\psi}\to\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pte^{i\beta\gamma^{5}}\,,\qquad b_{\mu}\to b_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\beta\,,

(2.3)

in which the right and left chiralities transform oppositely. By Noether’s
theorem these two classical invariances of the action
S_{f}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\mathscr{L}_{f}
at
m=0
imply the
conservation of both the electric and axial currents,

J^{\mu}=\frac{\delta S_{f}}{\delta A_{\mu}}=e\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi\,,\qquad\qquad J_{5}^{\mu}=\frac{\delta S_{f}}{\delta b_{\mu}}=\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\psi\,,

(2.4)

with

\hskip 32.72049pt\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}=0\,,\hskip 95.3169pt\partial_{\mu}J_{5}^{\mu}=2im\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi\qquad\text{(classically)}.

(2.5)

The
m=0
case thus has an apparent
U(1)^{\text{EM}}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry.

As is well-known, this apparent larger classical symmetry at
m=0
does not survive in the quantum theory,
since it turns out to be impossible to simultaneously satisfy the requirements of Lorentz invariance,

U(1)^{\text{EM}}
gauge invariance and
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
chiral invariance at the quantum many-particle
level
[
9
,
55
,
56
]
. This conflict of symmetries first appears
at the one-loop level of the triangle diagram of Fig.
1
.

k
p
q
J^{\mu}_{5}
J^{\alpha}
J^{\beta}

Fig.​ 1
:
The one-loop axial anomaly
\langle J_{5}^{\mu}J^{\alpha}J^{\beta}\rangle
triangle diagram, which is
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)

of (
A.4
) in momentum space. The solid lines represent the propagators of Dirac fermions
and the wavy lines external photon legs, which carry off four-momenta
p
and
q
from the electromagnetic
current vertices
J^{\alpha}
and
J^{\beta}
. The incoming four-momentum at the axial current vertex
J^{\mu}_{5}

is
k=p+q
by momentum conservation.

The triangle diagram
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
is naively
ultraviolet (UV) linearly divergent at large loop momenta or short distances, and hence is
a priori undefined
and in need of some further prescription. As reviewed in Appendix
Appendix A. The Chiral Anomaly in QED
4
and its IR Aspect
, it is rendered finite and well-defined by the requirements of:

(a)

Lorentz and parity/time reversal invariance of the vacuum or ground state (
A.4
);

(b)

Electric charge current conservation (
A.2
);

(c)

Bose symmetry (
A.5
) under interchange of the photon legs;

(d)

Real part obtained from imaginary part by unsubtracted dispersion (Kramers–Kronig) relation.

With these four requirements
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
does
not

satisfy axial or chiral invariance (
2.5
) in the massless fermion limit
m\to 0
.

In fact, contraction of
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
defined by requirements
(a)
-
(d)
with the external
momentum
k_{\mu}=p_{\mu}+q_{\mu}
entering at the axial vector vertex gives the well-defined result

k_{\mu}\,\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)={\cal A}\,\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)\,,

(2.6)

where

\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)\equiv\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}p_{\rho}q_{\sigma}=\upsilon^{\beta\alpha}(q,p)

(2.7)

and
{\cal A}
is most conveniently presented as an integral over Feynman parameters
x,y
in the form

{\cal A}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})=\frac{e^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(1-2\hskip 0.5ptm^{2}\,\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \frac{1}{D}\right),

(2.8)

where the denominator
D
in (
2.8
) is given by (
A.8
). The second term proportional to
m^{2}

is what would be expected from the axial vector divergence
\partial_{\mu}J_{5}^{\mu}=2im\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi
(
2.5
) following
from use of the on-shell Dirac equation for fermions of mass
m
,
cf.
(
A.10
)
[
57
]
.
The first term in (
2.8
), namely

{\cal A}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2}\!=\!0)=\frac{e^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\,,

(2.9)

which survives even in the case of massless fermions
m\to 0
, is the finite axial or chiral anomaly, since it
violates the expectation of the classical divergence of
\partial_{\mu}J_{5}^{\mu}
(
2.5
) vanishing at
m=0
.

The result (
2.8
)-(
2.9
) is equivalent to

\partial_{\mu}\langle J^{\mu}_{5}\rangle\Big|_{m=0}\equiv{\mathscr{A}}_{4}=\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\,\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F_{\mu\nu}=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\,\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}

(2.10)

in position space, in the presence of external electric
\boldsymbol{E}
and magnetic
\boldsymbol{B}
fields. Here

\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.5pt\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}
is the dual of
F_{\mu\nu}
, and

\alpha=e^{2}/4\pi\simeq 1/137.036
is the fine structure constant.

The determination of
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}
and (
2.8
) solely by the conditions

(a)
-
(d)
above shows that these symmetry requirements are all that are necessary and sufficient to determine the anomaly (
2.10
),
which is finite and independent of any UV divergences or renormalization. Thus the axial anomaly may be viewed
as a low energy or
macroscopic
feature of QFT, which is why it is relevant for EFT treatments of both
particle physics and condensed matter systems such as WSMs.

The triangle amplitude
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
for massless fermions may be decomposed as

\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\,\frac{k^{\mu}\!}{k^{2}\!}\ \epsilon^{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}p_{\rho}q_{\sigma}+\Gamma_{5\,\perp}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)\,,

(2.11)

where
\Gamma_{5\,\perp}^{\mu\alpha\beta}
is transverse,
k_{\mu}\Gamma_{5\,\perp}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)=0
, and hence non-anomalous,
while the first term in (
2.11
) which is responsible for the anomaly contains a massless
1/k^{2}

pole. Whereas the transverse part may (and does) receive all manner of radiative corrections from higher order
processes, the longitudinal anomalous pole contribution in (
2.11
) is protected from such
corrections by the Adler–Bardeen theorem
[
58
]
. This remarkable fact can be understood
to be a result of the topological character of the anomaly equation (
2.10
)
[
59
,
60
]
,
and another indication of its long distance properties. The topologically protected massless pole at

k^{2}=0
in the anomaly term describes a two-particle fermion/anti-fermion intermediate state in the triangle
diagram, with this fermionic pair propagating coherently and co-linearly at the speed of
light
[
61
]
,
cf.
Fig.
2
. This fermion pair state of opposite
helicities is just that of a massless
boson

[
37
]
.

e^{+}
e^{-}
J^{\mu}_{5}
J^{\alpha}
J^{\beta}

Fig.​ 2
:
The
e^{+}e^{-}
co-linear massless fermion pair state of opposite helicities from
the triangle diagram of Fig.
1
represented as a single massless effective boson.

This gapless bosonic mode is a collective excitation of the Fermi–Dirac sea, revealed first in one spatial
dimension in the Schwinger model of massless QED
2

[
62
]
, and in fermionic condensed
matter systems as Luttinger liquids
[
63
,
64
,
65
]
. Similar Luttinger
liquid-like behavior has been conjectured in higher dimensional fermion systems at low
temperatures
[
66
,
67
,
68
]
. It is the
1/k^{2}
anomaly pole in
(
2.11
) that provides the theoretical basis for this conjecture to be physically realized in WSMs,
with the Fermi velocity
v_{F}
replacing the speed of light
c
, and the fermion pairing to be
analogous to the Cooper pairs of a superfluid condensate
[
36
]
.

The bosonic excitation arising directly from the axial anomaly itself provides a new realization of Goldstone’s
theorem, anomaly symmetry breaking (ASB), discussed in Sec.
9
, without (so far) any explicit
reference to a bosonic order parameter expectation value violating the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry, as in the
usual case of SSB. We shall see that despite the fact that the axial anomaly modifies the WT identities of the
theory whereas SSB preserves them, the two apparently distinct situations of ASB and SSB are very closely related
and can be described by the same low energy EFT, as a superfluid with a Goldstone sound mode that is an
axion excitation in a WSM.

3
Anomaly Effective Action and Superfluid EFT of Weyl Nodes Displaced in Energy

Since the axial current
J^{\mu}_{5}
is the variation of the action with respect to the external
axial potential
b_{\mu}
, the longitudinal projection of (
2.11
) is the variation of the non-local 1PI effective action
(i)

[
37
,
69
,
70
]

\Gamma_{\rm anom}^{\rm NL}[A,b]=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}y\,b_{\mu}(x)\,\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{x}^{\mu}\,\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,^{-1}_{xy}\,{\mathscr{A}}_{4}(y)=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}y\,\big[b_{\mu}\hskip 0.5pt\partial^{\mu}\big]_{x}\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,^{-1}_{xy}\,\big[F_{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.5pt\widetilde{F}^{\alpha\beta}\big]_{y}

(3.1)

in position space, where
\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,^{-1}_{xy}=\frac{1\,}{4\pi^{2}}\,(x-y)^{-2}
denotes the massless scalar propagator
inverse of the wave operator
\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,=\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}=-\partial_{t}^{2}+\nabla^{2}
in
3+1
spacetime dimensions and
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
the
axial anomaly given by (
2.10
). The effective action (
3.1
) is non-local, although its variation with
respect to
b_{\mu}
which gives
\langle J^{\mu}_{5}\rangle
, and subsequent acting with
\partial/\partial x^{\mu}
, reproduces the local result
(
2.10
).

The non-local 1PI effective action (
3.1
) can also be expressed in a local form

\mathcal{S}_{\rm anom}[\eta;A,b]=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\Big[\big(\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}\big)\hskip 0.5ptJ_{5}^{\mu}+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\Big]

(3.2)

upon the introduction of the local pseudoscalar potential
\eta
. The variation of (
3.2
) with respect
to
\eta
reproduces the anomaly (
2.10
), while the variation of (
3.2
) with respect to the hydrodynamic
current
J_{5}^{\mu}
produces the constraint
\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}=0
. Solving this constraint for
\eta
gives
\eta=-\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,^{-1}\partial^{\mu}b_{\mu}
,
and substituting this back into (
3.2
) reproduces the non-local action (
3.1
) with its massless pole,
after integration by parts. This variational method with respect to a current in which no reference to the underlying
QFT variables
\psi
is made, is employed for effective actions of fluid hydrodynamics
(iii)
, in
which context
\eta
is called a
Clebsch potential

[
71
]
.

Note that apart from the anomaly term
\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
, (
3.2
) depends upon the linear combination

\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}
, which is invariant under
\eta\to\eta-\beta,b_{\mu}\to b_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\beta
. This is
a suggestive remnant of the local
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry (
2.3
), although the fermions themselves
do not appear explicitly in the anomaly effective action (
3.2
). The fluid form has only bosonic
variables, and a fluid variational principle in which only the full current
J^{\mu}_{5}
is varied, not
its fermionic constituents. Thus it is not immediately apparent from (
3.2
) to what complex
bosonic variable
\Phi
the phase
\eta
corresponds, or how its magnitude
|\Phi|
is related to the underlying
fermion QFT.

On the constraint
\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}=0
, the
\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
term alone in (
3.2
) is the form of the effective
action of the axial anomaly that can be shown to be responsible for the anomalous Hall conductance, as well as the
chiral magnetic and chiral separation effects
[
13
,
36
]
. Further, if

2\hskip 0.5ptb^{\mu}=(2\hskip 0.5ptb^{0},2\hskip 0.5ptb^{i})
is the difference in the energies and momenta of the two Weyl nodes
of a WSM respectively, illustrated in Fig.
3
, then

\eta=-x^{\mu}b_{\mu}=t\hskip 0.5ptb^{0}-\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{x}}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}
is the chiral phase obtained by a fixed chiral
rotation on the fermions through Fujikawa’s method
[
72
,
73
]
, and (
3.2
),
giving the effective action of the axial anomaly for such WSMs in
[
2
,
13
,
15
]
.

\boldsymbol{k}
E
-b^{0}
-\boldsymbol{b}
b^{0}
\boldsymbol{b}

Fig.​ 3
:
Two Weyl cones separated by
(2b^{0},2\boldsymbol{b})
in momentum space. Here we assume zero charge
chemical potential, so the Weyl cones are filled up to the Fermi energy, as indicated by the fill pattern.
We also neglect the effects of non-linear interactions at energies
\Lambda_{W}
far from the Weyl nodes, so that
the EFT description applies for energy excitations and
|2b^{0}|\ll\Lambda_{W}
.

In the WSM literature, the phase
\theta=-2\eta
multiplying the axial anomaly
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
in (
3.2
) is called
an ‘axion.’ However, in QFT the axion of
[
28
,
29
]
is a
propagating
pseudoscalar
field, not simply a chiral rotation angle, of a fixed
b_{\mu}
axial potential characterizing Weyl nodes in
their equilibrium configuration. To obtain a true propagating axion in a WSM the two Weyl nodes of
Fig.
3
must be allowed to fluctuate around their equilibrium values, and a kinetic
term for
\eta
must be added. Such a kinetic term may just be assumed to be present in the EFT,
as IR relevant terms in the Wilsonian sense
(ii)
,
e.g.

[
74
,
14
,
75
]
.
However the origin of this kinetic term is not specified in that case and its coefficient must be
treated as an unknown free parameter that can only be fixed by experiment in such an approach.

On the other hand, continuing with the
Fluid
description
(iii)
of free massless fermions with
displaced Fermi levels for left- and right-handed Weyl fermions automatically provides just such a
kinetic term for small deviations from equilibrium, with coefficient fixed by the equilibrium equation of state,
once the energy density
\varepsilon(n_{5})
of the fermions as a general function of the chiral number density

n_{5}
is taken into account. Adding this non-anomalous
-\varepsilon(n_{5})
term to the local anomaly effective
action (
3.2
) results in the fluid effective action

\mathcal{S}_{\rm Fluid}=\mathcal{S}_{\rm anom}[\eta;A,b]-\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\varepsilon(n_{5})=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\Big[\big(\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}\big)J_{5}^{\mu}+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}-\varepsilon(n_{5})\Big]

(3.3)

for the massless fermion system at finite
n_{5}
. The variation of this effective action with respect to

J_{5}^{\mu}
is now non-trivial and leads to

\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}-\left(\!\frac{d\varepsilon}{dn_{5}}\!\right)\left(\!\frac{dn_{5}}{dJ_{5}^{\mu}}\!\right)=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad J_{5}^{\mu}=-\frac{n_{5}}{\mu_{5}}\,\big(\partial^{\mu}\eta+b^{\mu}\big)

(3.4)

instead of the constraint
\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}=0
, and where the Lorentz frame-invariant definition of

n_{5}^{2}=-J_{5}^{\mu}J_{5\,\mu}
has been used. The quantity
\mu_{5}
, likewise invariantly defined by

\mu_{5}=\frac{d\varepsilon}{dn_{5}}\,,\qquad\mu_{5}^{2}=-\big(\partial^{\mu}\eta+b^{\mu}\big)\big(\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}\big)\,,

(3.5)

carries the interpretation of the axial chemical potential of the chiral effective fluid. Using these
definitions, (
3.3
) can be expressed in the form

\mathcal{S}_{\rm eff,fluid}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\big(\mu_{5}n_{5}-\varepsilon+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\big)=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\big(P+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\big)\,,

(3.6)

where
P=\mu_{5}n_{5}-\varepsilon
is the equilibrium pressure of the fluid at zero temperature.

For a WSM whose two Weyl nodes of opposite chirality are displaced in energy and momentum space by
2\hskip 0.5ptb^{0}
and
2\hskip 0.5ptb_{i}

respectively, as in Fig.
3
, in the rest frame of the material in equilibrium all time derivatives vanish,
whereas
J^{0}_{5}=n_{5}
is non-vanishing. Thus when the non-zero
\varepsilon(n_{5})
and
\mu_{5}
of the fluid in (
3.3
) are taken
into account, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}=-\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{x}}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}

\displaystyle\,,\qquad\qquad\dot{\bar{\eta}}=0\,,

(3.7a)

\displaystyle\mu_{5}=b_{0}

\displaystyle\,,\qquad\qquad\bar{J}^{0}_{5}=n_{5}\,,

(3.7b)

and the electric current

J^{\mu}=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\hskip 0.5pt\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{\nu}\eta\quad\Rightarrow\quad\bar{\!\boldsymbol{J}}=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{B}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{\bar{\eta}}=0

(3.8)

and axial current
\bar{\!\boldsymbol{J}}_{\!5}=0
vanish in equilibrium from (
3.4
) and (
3.7a
),
including also in the presence of a background magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}
. In equilibrium with
\boldsymbol{E}=0
, the
anomaly
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
also vanishes, and
\partial_{\mu}\bar{J}_{5}^{\mu}=0
. Note that as a result of (
3.4
) and using
\bar{J}^{0}_{5}=n_{5}
and
\bar{\!\boldsymbol{J}}_{\!5}=0
in equilibrium, we find
\dot{\bar{\eta}}=\mu_{5}-b_{0}
, and the anomalous electric current is zero in equilibrium, consistent with the literature
[
76
]
.

Now since
\eta
is a dynamical field in the effective fluid description and
J^{\mu}_{5}
depends on

\eta
through (
3.4
), small fluctuations
\delta\eta(t,{\bf x})
from equilibrium can be considered
and these will generate small fluctuations in
\mu_{5}
and
J^{\mu}_{5}
, according to

\displaystyle\delta\mu_{5}

\displaystyle=\delta\dot{\eta}\,,

(3.9a)

\displaystyle\delta J_{5}^{0}

\displaystyle=\delta n_{5}=\frac{\delta n_{5}}{\delta\mu_{5}}\,\delta\mu_{5}=\frac{\delta n_{5}}{\delta\mu_{5}}\,\delta\dot{\eta}\,,

(3.9b)

\displaystyle\delta J^{i}_{5}

\displaystyle=-\frac{n_{5}}{\mu_{5}}\,\nabla^{i}\,\delta\eta\,,

(3.9c)

for the chiral current components and their departure from equilibrium. To first order in these departures
from equilibrium the variation in (
3.9b
) may be replaced by its value from the equilibrium equation of state,

\frac{\delta n_{5}}{\delta\mu_{5}}\simeq\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\,,

(3.10)

where we drop overbars on the equilibrium values of
\mu_{5},n_{5}
, which are independent of
t,\boldsymbol{x}
.
Now using the definition of the sound speed in the fluid

v_{s}^{2}=\frac{dp}{d\varepsilon}=\frac{n_{5}\,d\mu_{5}}{\mu_{5}\,dn_{5}}=\frac{n_{5}}{\mu_{5}}\left(\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\right)^{\!-1}\,,

(3.11)

the axial anomaly equation
\partial_{\mu}(\delta J^{\mu}_{5})=\delta{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
for the linear variations in (
3.9
)
may be expressed in the form

\partial_{\mu}(\delta J^{\mu}_{5})=\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}-v_{s}^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\right)\delta\eta=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\,\delta\big(F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\big)\,.

(3.12)

This follows also from (
3.3
), expanded to quadratic order in the departures from equilibrium,
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{\rm eff,fluid}

\displaystyle\simeq\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\,\delta\eta\left(\!-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+v_{s}^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\right)\delta\eta+\delta\eta\,\delta{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\bigg]\,.

(3.13)

If the anomaly term
\delta{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
is assumed to be of the same order of small variation
\delta\eta
from equilibrium,
the coupling of
\delta\eta
to electromagnetism would need to be considered. However since
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\sim\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}

is quadratic in the EM fields, this will only be the case if there is a non-zero background field of either

\boldsymbol{E}
or
\boldsymbol{B}
. The latter is considered in the next section. Otherwise if both
\boldsymbol{E}
and
\boldsymbol{B}

are flucutations from the vacuum of
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{B}=0
, the last term in (
3.13
) is third order in
fluctuations and the EM influence on
\delta\eta
can be neglected. This last term in (
3.13
)
will nevertheless generate an electric current as in (
3.8
) that acts as source for Maxwell’s equations.

Eq. (
3.12
) shows that the effective action of the axial anomaly, expressed in the hydrodynamic
fluid form (
3.2
)-(
3.3
), predicts the existence of a gapless pseudoscalar chiral density wave (CDW)
that couples linearly to
F\widetilde{F}
and therefore may be identified as an axion for a WSM arising from displaced
Weyl nodes, as in Fig.
3
, that fluctuate from their equilibrium value. This axionic CDW
arises from the axial anomaly itself, independently of direct fermion self-interactions. For the specific
example of free Weyl fermions we have the zero temperature equilibrium relations

\displaystyle n_{5}=\left(J_{5}^{\mu}J_{5\,\mu}\right)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}=2\!\int_{|\boldsymbol{p}|\leqslant\mu_{5}}\frac{d^{3}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}=\frac{\mu_{5}^{3}}{3\pi^{2}}\,,

(3.14a)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(n_{5})=2\!\int_{|\boldsymbol{p}|\leqslant\mu_{5}}\frac{d^{3}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,|\boldsymbol{p}|=\frac{\mu_{5}^{4}}{4\pi^{2}}=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}$}\,(3\pi^{2})^{\frac{1}{3}}\,n_{5}^{\frac{4}{3}}\,,

(3.14b)

\displaystyle P(\mu_{5})=\mu_{5}n_{5}-\varepsilon=\frac{\mu_{5}^{4}}{12\pi^{2}}=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{3}$}\,,\qquad\quad\frac{dP}{d\mu_{5}}=n_{5}

(3.14c)

and the speed of the CDW,
v_{s}
in this specific case of free fermions with Weyl nodes displaced
in energy and momentum, is given by

v_{s}^{2}\big|_{\text{free}}=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}$}\hskip 0.5ptv_{F}^{2}\,,

(3.15)

where we have reinserted the Fermi velocity
v_{F}
which plays the role of the speed of light in the
relativistic (gapless) Weyl fermion Hamiltonian.

These simple considerations following from the axial anomaly itself in its effective fluid description
(
3.6
) predict the existence of a true dynamical and propagating collective axion excitation of
a WSM with a pair of Weyl nodes displaced in energy. In Sec.
9
we shall show that the appearance
of this gapless mode of a WSM can be understood as a consequence of an extension of Goldstone’s theorem to
the case of anomaly symmetry breaking. Physically, it is a collective mode arising from small
perturbations of the Fermi surface of a WSM, with the fermion pairing arising through electromagnetism and
the axial anomaly itself, as in Fig.
2
. At zero temperature and in the absence of defects this
CDW axionic mode is dissipationless, and the effective action (
3.3
) of the WSM fluid is that of
a relativistic superfluid
[
77
]
.

4
The Axion for WSMs in a Constant Uniform Magnetic Field: Dimensional Reduction

As a second example of the application of the hydrodynamic EFT to a WSM, consider a WSM,
for free or very weakly self-interacting fermions, and with no offset in energy of the Weyl nodes,

i.e.

b^{0}=0
, but placed in a constant, uniform magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}=B\bf\hat{x}
.
In this case the triangle diagram and vertex
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}
reduces to its longitudinal component
only and the effective action restricted to excitations along
\boldsymbol{B}
becomes exact
[
36
]
.
The details of this dimensional reduction in a constant uniform magnetic field with the fermions
in their lowest Landau level (LLL) are reviewed in Appendix
Appendix B. The Axial Anomaly for WSMs in a Constant Uniform Magnetic Field
for the convenience of the reader.

The energy density of chiral fermions in their LLL in
3+1
spacetime dimensions
\varepsilon(n_{5})
can be related
to the energy density of fermions in
1+1
spacetime dimensions
\varepsilon_{2}
by

\varepsilon(n_{5})=\frac{eB}{2\pi}\,\varepsilon_{2}(\tilde{n})=\frac{\pi^{2}}{eB}\,n_{5}^{2}\,,

(4.1)

where

n_{5}=\frac{eB}{2\pi}\,\tilde{n}

(4.2)

is the
3+1
dimensional chiral number density of fermions in the LLL, in terms of

\tilde{n}=(-\tilde{\jmath}^{\hskip 0.5pta}\tilde{\jmath}_{a})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad a=0,1\,,\qquad\varepsilon_{2}(\tilde{n})=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}$}\tilde{n}^{2}

(4.3)

the chiral density and energy density of
1+1
dimensional fermions restricted to moving in the

\hat{\bf x}
direction along
\boldsymbol{B}
. The 2D chiral current and number density in (
4.3
)
are denoted by
\tilde{\jmath}_{a}
and
\tilde{n}
respectively.

Factoring out a common factor of the electron number density per unit area
eB/2\pi
in the LLL leads to
the 2D effective action

S_{{\rm anom},B}=\frac{eB}{2\pi}\!\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}{\bf x}_{\perp}\!\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}x\,\big[(\partial^{a}\eta+b^{a})\tilde{\jmath}_{a}+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{{\mathscr{A}}}_{2}-\varepsilon_{2}(\tilde{n})\big]

(4.4)

of a 2D chiral superfluid, where the two dimensional axial anomaly is

{\mathscr{A}}_{2}=\frac{e}{2\pi}\,\epsilon^{ab}F_{ab}=\frac{eE}{\pi}\,,

(4.5)

where the
a,b
indices range over
0,1
only and the electric field
E=F_{10}
is also in the

\hat{\bf x}
direction along
\boldsymbol{B}
. The 2D axial chemical potential

\tilde{\mu}=\frac{d\varepsilon_{2}}{d\tilde{n}}=(-\partial^{a}\eta\,\partial_{a}\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\pi\tilde{n}=\mu_{5}=\frac{d\varepsilon}{dn_{5}}

(4.6)

is in fact the same as that evaluated in 4D. Note that in 2D the axial anomaly (
4.5
) is linear
in the electric field, so that variations in
{\mathscr{A}}_{2}
will be of the same order as those of
\eta
in
2D, or in 4D as a result of dimensional reduction in a classical background
\boldsymbol{B}
field.

Apart from the overall factor of
\frac{eB}{2\pi}\!\int\!d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}{\bf x}_{\perp}
, the 2D effective action
in (
4.4
) for excitations along the
\boldsymbol{B}
direction is thus
[
36
]

S_{\rm 2D}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}x\,\big[(\partial^{a}\eta+b^{a})\tilde{\jmath}_{a}+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{{\mathscr{A}}}_{2}-\varepsilon_{2}(\tilde{n})\big]=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}x\Big[-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}$}\,\partial^{a}\chi\,\partial_{a}\chi-\,(eE+\partial^{a}b_{a})\,\chi\Big]

(4.7)

in terms of a local pseudoscalar boson field
\chi
, related to the chiral phase
\eta
by

\eta=-\pi\chi+\frac{\theta}{2}\to-\pi\chi\,,

(4.8)

where the arbitrary constant
\theta
phase angle associated with the anomaly is set to zero here.
The 2D effective fluid action (
4.7
) for excitations parallel to the
\boldsymbol{B}
field
is then recognized as the bosonic effective action of the fermionic sector of the Schwinger model,

i.e.
QED
2
of massless fermions
[
62
,
70
,
36
]

in which the gauge potential in
\epsilon^{ab}F_{ab}/2=\epsilon^{ab}\partial_{a}A_{b}
of the 2D model
has been replaced by
eA_{a}+\epsilon_{a}{\!}^{c}b_{c}
of the dimensionally reduced 4D theory.

The variations

\tilde{\jmath}^{\hskip 0.5pta}=\frac{\delta}{\delta b_{a}}\,S_{\rm 2D}[\chi;A,b]=\partial^{a}\chi\,,\qquad j^{a}=\frac{\delta}{\delta A_{a}}\,S_{\rm 2D}[\chi;A,b]=-\epsilon^{ab}\partial_{b}\chi

(4.9)

express the bosonization rules for the 2D chiral and electric currents. The extremization

\frac{\delta}{\delta\chi}\,S_{\rm 2D}[\chi;A,b]=\pi\,\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,_{2}\chi-(E+\partial^{a}b_{a})=\pi\big(\partial^{a}\tilde{\jmath}_{a}-{\mathscr{A}}_{2}\big)=0

(4.10)

recovers the 2D axial anomaly (
B.8
), and the 2D wave operator
\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,_{2}
shows that the
\chi
(or
\eta
)
boson is a true propagating axion degree of freedom as a result of the anomaly itself. The Green’s function of
the 2D wave operator
\left(\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,^{-1}_{2}\right)_{xy}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\ln(x-y)^{2}
in (
4.11
) is the massless
scalar propagator in
d=2
spacetime dimensions, describing propagation along the
\boldsymbol{B}
direction.
The non-local form of (
4.7
) is

\displaystyle S_{\rm 2D}^{\text{NL}}[A,b]=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}x\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}x^{\prime}\,\big(\partial^{a}b_{a}+E\big)_{x}\left(\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,^{-1}_{2}\right)_{xx^{\prime}}\big(\partial^{a}b_{a}+E\big)_{x^{\prime}}

(4.11)

assuming the electric field
{\bf E}=E(t,x)\,\bf\hat{x}
is also along the
\boldsymbol{B}
direction and
independent of the transverse coordinates
\bf y
.

By the Gauss law
\partial_{x}E=\rho
, an electric field

E=-\frac{2\alpha B}{\pi}\,\eta

(4.12)

parallel to the
\boldsymbol{B}
field direction is induced. Finally, variation of
(
4.4
) with respect
to
\eta
reproduces the axial anomaly back in
3+1
dimensions

\partial_{\mu}J_{5}^{\mu}=\frac{eB}{2\pi^{2}}\big[\big(\partial_{t}^{2}-\partial_{x}^{2}\big)\eta+\partial^{a}b_{a}\big]=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\,EB=-\left(\frac{2\alpha B}{\pi}\right)^{2}\eta\,,

(4.13)

which is equivalent to the linear wave equation

\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\partial_{x}^{2}+\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\,eB\right)\eta=-\partial^{a}b_{a}

(4.14)

with the mass gap
M^{2}=2\alpha eB/\pi
. The energy-momentum offset of the Weyl modes may be set
to zero,
b^{a}=0
, at this point. It has been retained in (
4.14
) only to illustrate
that such an offset which is varying in time or space acts as a source for the axion phase
mode aligned with the magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}
.

The wave equation (
4.14
) describes chiral density waves (CDWs) and chiral magnetic waves
(CMWs) here propagating at the speed of sound
v_{s}=v_{F}=1
, with the quantum numbers of an axion.
The CDW/CMW
\eta
wave arises as a gapless bosonic mode of the axial anomaly describing excitations of
the one dimensional Fermi surface of chiral fermions in the LLL, and is a direct consequence of massless
anomaly
1/k^{2}
pole. However, as a result of the dimensional reduction to 2D, the 2D anomaly (
4.5
)
and
E
in (
4.12
) is of the same order as
\eta
itself. Thus in this case the interaction
of
\eta
and
{\mathscr{A}}_{2}
cannot be neglected as in the 4D case of Sec.
3
, and as a result
the axion mode becomes massive (gapped) by its electromagnetic interactions, exactly as the Schwinger
boson does in the 2D Schwinger model, and analogously to the Anderson–Higgs mechanism in the Standard Model.
Thus by this dimensional reduction, a WSM subjected to a magnetic field gives rise to CDW/CMW axionic excitations
along the
\boldsymbol{B}
direction, due to the axial anomaly itself, and furnishes a second example of ASB in
the absence of any direct fermion-fermion interactions, other than electromagnetic ones.

5
Fermion Self-Interactions and SSB of Chiral Symmetry

In the condensed matter literature on WSMs, it is usually supposed that the fermion excitations at the Weyl nodes may
be subject to a generic four-fermion interaction of the Nambu–Jona-Lasino (NJL) type
[
14
,
34
,
35
]

\mathscr{L}_{\text{NJL}}=\tfrac{1}{4}\hskip 0.5ptG\hskip 0.5pt\big[(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}-(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi)^{2}\big]

(5.1)

of unspecified origin in the material. This NJL Lagrangian is invariant under the global
U(1)^{\rm EM}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
chiral symmetry
\psi\to e^{i\alpha+i\beta\gamma^{5}}\psi
just as the massless fermion Lagrangian
(
2.1
) is at the classical level. For
G>0
the interaction (
5.1
)
is attractive. Since this fermion self-coupling
G
has mass dimension
-2
, the NJL theory is non-renormalizable in the UV
and must come equipped with a cutoff at some high energy scale
\Lambda
, corresponding to a short distance cutoff
1/\Lambda
.

The fermion self-coupling
G
is most conveniently handled by introducing a two-component charge neutral scalar field

\Phi=\phi_{1}+i\phi_{2}\gamma^{5}\,,\qquad\Phi^{{\dagger}}=\phi_{1}-i\phi_{2}\gamma^{5}

(5.2)

in the Dirac matrix space, with both scalar (
\phi_{1}
) and pseudoscalar (
\phi_{2}
) components, coupled to the fermions
through the Yukawa interaction

\mathscr{L}_{Y}=-g\bar{\psi}\Phi\psi=-g\bar{\psi}\big(\phi_{1}+i\phi_{2}\gamma^{5}\big)\psi\,,

(5.3)

where
g
is a dimensionless Yukawa coupling. Since the fermions transform under a

U(1)^{\text{EM}}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
phase transformation by (
2.3
), the interaction
(
5.3
) is invariant under this transformation provided

\Phi\to e^{-2i\beta\gamma^{5}}\,\Phi=\big(\phi_{1}\cos 2\beta+\phi_{2}\sin 2\beta\big)+i\big(\phi_{2}\cos 2\beta-\phi_{1}\sin 2\beta\big)\gamma^{5}\,,

(5.4)

i.e.

\Phi
is electrically neutral but carries a chiral charge that is opposite in sign
to that of
\psi
and twice as large. The interaction Lagrangian

\mathscr{L}_{\text{int}}=-g\bar{\psi}\big(\phi_{1}+i\phi_{2}\gamma^{5}\big)\psi-\frac{g^{2}}{G}\big(\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2}\big)

(5.5)

is equivalent to the original four-fermion NJL interaction Lagrangian (
5.1
) after
a Hubbard–Stratonovich transformation, obtained by extremizing
\mathscr{L}_{\text{int}}
with respect
to
\phi_{i}
, yielding

g\phi_{1}=-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}G\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\psi\,,\qquad g\phi_{2}=-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}$}G\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi\,,\qquad\text{(classically)}

(5.6)

and substituting the result into
\mathscr{L}_{\text{int}}
, whereupon
\mathscr{L}_{\text{NJL}}
is recovered.
Thus, the fermion bilinears of the NJL model are replaced by the
\Phi
boson field in this description.

In the NJL model the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry is spontaneously broken,
\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle\neq 0
, and
the fermion acquires a mass gap
m
if the self-coupling
G
exceeds a certain critical value
G_{c}
. This
follows from the solution of the minimization of the effective potential for
\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle
, which leads to
condition

2m\left(\frac{1}{G}-\frac{1}{G_{c}}\right)=-\frac{m^{3}}{4\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\,,\qquad G_{c}\equiv\frac{8\pi^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\,,

(5.7)

in terms of the ultraviolet cutoff
\Lambda\gg m
. If
G<G_{c}
, the only solution of (
5.7
)
for real
m
is
m=0
, the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry remains unbroken and the fermion remains
massless. On the other hand, if
G>G_{c}
a second solution appears with
m>0
, given by

\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}=1-\frac{G_{c}}{G}>0\,,\qquad\text{for}\qquad G>G_{c}\,,

(5.8)

in which the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry is spontaneously broken and the fermion acquires a mass gap
m>0
. By
computing the second derivative of the scalar effective potential with respect to
\phi_{1}
, it is straightforward to show
that the symmetric solution at
m=0
becomes unstable,
cf.
(
C.6
), and the SSB solution (
5.8
)
has a lower energy if
G>G_{c}
. Thus, for sufficiently strong fermion self-interactions the fermions become gapped with

m>0
, and the ground state breaks the
U(1)^{\text{EM}}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry down to
U(1)^{\text{EM}}
.
As a result, Goldstone’s theorem for the spontaneous breaking of a global symmetry assures us that there is a gapless
Goldstone boson corresponding to the chiral phase of the
\Phi
field, in more than one spatial dimension.

These statements and the condition for SSB of
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
have their analogs in the effective potential
of the bosonic field
\Phi
, once it is provided with a quartic self-interaction term
\lambda\,\hskip 0.5pt\textrm{tr}\,\Phi^{4}
, so that
its energy is bounded from below. The
U(1)^{\rm EM}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
invariant quartic potential

\displaystyle V(\sigma)=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{2}$}\,\sigma^{2}+\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{4}$}\,\sigma^{4}

(5.9)

depends only upon
\sigma=|\Phi|
and the parameters
\kappa
and
\lambda>0
. The minimum of this potential at

V^{\prime}(\bar{\sigma})=\kappa\bar{\sigma}+\lambda\bar{\sigma}^{3}=0\,,

(5.10)

which admits the solution

{\bar{\sigma}}=\sqrt{\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\!-\kappa\,}{\,\lambda}$}}\,\neq 0\quad{\rm if}\quad\kappa<0\,,

(5.11)

so that the
\Phi
field develops an expectation value
\langle\phi_{1}\rangle\equiv\bar{\sigma}\neq 0
in the ground state
that spontaneously breaks the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry if
\kappa<0
. Comparing the quadratic term in (
5.9
)
to that of the fermion loop in (
C.29
) shows that

\kappa=2g^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{G}-\frac{1}{G_{c}}\Big)

(5.12)

and the condition for SSB in the scalar theory
\kappa<0
coincides with the condition
G>G_{c}
in the
fermionic NJL model. Upon identifying also

\lambda=\frac{g^{4}}{4\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}

(5.13)

from the quartic term induced by the fermion loop in (
C.29
), we then find that

m^{2}=g^{2}{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}=-g^{2}\,\frac{\kappa}{\lambda}=\frac{8\pi^{2}}{\ln(\Lambda^{2}/m^{2})}\,\Big(\frac{1}{G_{c}}-\frac{1}{G}\Big)>0\qquad\text{for}\qquad G>G_{c}\,,

(5.14)

which is equivalent to the gap equation (
5.7
) in the fermionic NJL model with cutoff
\Lambda
,
demonstrating the consistency of the scalar field description with the original NJL fermionic one.

Since the scalar
\Phi
defined by (
5.3
) and (
5.6
) has mass dimension one, the quartic potential
term
\lambda\,\hskip 0.5pt\textrm{tr}\Phi^{4}
and kinetic terms for
\Phi
, Tr
(\partial^{\mu}\Phi^{\dagger}\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{\mu}\Phi)
, are dimension four.
Although neither are generally considered in the condensed matter literature on WSMs, these terms are IR
relevant in the sense of the Wilson renormalization group and are necessarily generated also, with
coefficients logarithmically dependent upon
\Lambda
, once the fermion one-loop quantum corrections to the NJL
model are considered,
cf.
Appendix
Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass
.

The classical
U(1)^{\rm EM}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry (
2.2
)-(
2.3
), prior to
consideration of the anomaly dictates that the kinetic terms for
\Phi
must occur in the invariant combination

\textrm{tr}\big[\big(\partial^{\mu}\Phi^{\dagger}-2ib^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\Phi^{\dagger}\big)\big(\partial_{\mu}\Phi+2ib_{\mu}\gamma^{5}\Phi\big)\big]

(5.15)

and that the kinetic terms for the
A_{\mu}
and
b_{\mu}=A^{5}_{\mu}
potentials involve only their respective field
strength tensors, namely
F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}
and
F^{5}_{\mu\nu}F^{5\,\mu\nu}
should also be included for a UV
complete theory in general. A mixed
F_{\alpha\beta}F^{5\,\alpha\beta}
term is disallowed by the discrete symmetries
of charge conjugation or parity spatial reflection.

These terms are generated by fermion loop integrations, with a logarithmic dependence upon the UV cutoff
\Lambda
,
as shown in Appendix
Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass
, just as one would be expect by power counting and the
U(1)^{\rm EM}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
classical symmetry. A UV renormalizable effective theory must contain these terms from the very beginning. In such a UV completion of the EFT
the couplings
\kappa,\lambda
and
g
and the fermion mass gap
m
will all be be finite renormalized parameters independent
of the UV cutoff
\Lambda
, to be fixed by experiment, and the scalar potential (
5.9
) can be treated at tree level,
as in the classical Landau theory of phase transitions
[
46
]
.
This is the approach we take in the next section.

6
A Renormalizable Theory for WSMs Encompassing both ASB and SSB

The effective Lagrangian in the two cases of free or weakly interacting fermions and strongly self-interacting
fermions with SSB can be derived as different limits of one and the same UV renormalizable classical theory with Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\text{cl}}=-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}$}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pt\gamma^{\mu}\big(i\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}+eA_{\mu}+b_{\mu}\gamma^{5}\big)\psi-g\bar{\psi}\Phi\psi+\mathscr{L}_{\Phi}\,,

(6.1)

with the bosonic Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}$}\,{\rm tr}\big[\big(\partial^{\mu}\Phi^{\dagger}-2i\hskip 0.5ptb^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\Phi^{\dagger}\big)\big(\partial_{\mu}\Phi+2i\hskip 0.5ptb_{\mu}\gamma^{5}\Phi\big)\big]-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{8}$}\,{\rm tr}\big(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi\big)-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{32}$}\,{\rm tr}\big(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}\big(\partial_{\mu}\phi_{1}-2\hskip 0.5ptb_{\mu}\phi_{2}\big)^{2}-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}\big(\partial_{\mu}\phi_{2}+2\hskip 0.5ptb_{\mu}\phi_{1}\big)^{2}-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{2}$}\,\big(\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2}\big)-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{4}$}\hskip 0.5pt\big(\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}\,,

(6.2)

where we keep all possible relevant terms up to dimension four, fully invariant under the

U(1)\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
and Lorentz symmetries. The couplings
g,\kappa,\lambda
will now be arbitrary
renormalized (
i.e.
UV cutoff independent) parameters that ultimately would have to be fixed by experiment
in any given WSM. The fermions are taken to be massless initially, with any mass generated only through
the Yukawa interaction, and the scalar
\Phi
developing a vacuum expectation value

\langle\Phi\rangle=\langle\phi_{1}\rangle\neq 0
.

As already discussed, one should also allow for kinetic terms for the axial vector potential, such as

F_{5}^{\mu\nu}F_{5\hskip 0.5pt\mu\nu}/f_{5}^{2}
for the transverse part of
b_{\mu}
, with an independent normalization and
chiral coupling
f_{5}^{2}
analogous to the electromagnetic coupling
e
of
\mathscr{L}_{\text{cl}}
. Anticipating
that the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry will be broken by the axial anomaly, a kinetic term
(\partial^{\mu}b_{\mu})^{2}

for the longitudinal component with another independent coupling as well as a mass term
b^{\mu}b_{\mu}
could also
be allowed in (
6.1
). However, once the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry is explicitly broken by the axial
anomaly, there is nothing preventing this mass term from being large, of the order of the cutoff scale
\Lambda
, in
which case all the components of the axial vector potential will be gapped and play no role in the low energy
EFT at long wavelength scales. Thus we omit from the effective Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}_{\text{cl}}
of (
6.1
)
all such kinetic and mass terms involving the axial potential
b_{\mu}
from the outset.

The
U(1)^{\rm EM}\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry of the classical action

S_{\text{cl}}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\mathscr{L}_{\text{cl}}
, with (
6.1
), (
6.2
)
results in the electromagnetic and axial currents

\displaystyle J^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta S_{\text{cl}}}{\delta A_{\mu}}=e\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi\,,

(6.3)

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta S_{\text{cl}}}{\delta b_{\mu}}=\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\psi+4\,\phi_{2}\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial^{\hskip 0.5pt\mu}\phi_{1}-4\hskip 0.5ptb^{\mu}(\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2})\,,

(6.4)

being conserved by Noether’s theorem, independently of SSB or a non-zero fermion mass, upon use of the
classical

equations of motion following from
S_{\text{cl}}
,
i.e.
before any consideration of the axial anomaly. Defining
the polar representation for
\Phi
,

\displaystyle\Phi=\hskip 0.5pt\sigma\exp(2i\hskip 0.5pt\zeta\gamma^{5})\,,

(6.5)

the bosonic part of the effective action (
6.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\partial^{\mu}\sigma\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{\mu}\sigma-2\hskip 0.5pt\sigma^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\big(\partial^{\mu}\zeta+b^{\mu}\big)\big(\partial_{\mu}\zeta+b_{\mu}\big)-V(\sigma)\,,

(6.6)

and the axial current has both the fermionic and bosonic contributions

J^{\mu}_{5}=\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\psi-4\hskip 0.5pt\sigma^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\big(\partial^{\mu}\zeta+b^{\mu}\big)\equiv J^{\mu}_{5}[\psi]+J^{\mu}_{5}[\Phi]\,,

(6.7)

where

J^{\mu}_{5}[\Phi]=4\,\phi_{2}\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial^{\hskip 0.5pt\mu}\phi_{1}-4\hskip 0.5ptb^{\mu}(\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2})=-4\hskip 0.5pt\sigma^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\big(\partial^{\mu}\zeta+b^{\mu}\big)

(6.8)

is the bosonic part of the axial current.

Note that the bosonic terms in both (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) depend on the chiral phase
\zeta
of (
6.5
)
only through the combination
\partial_{\mu}\zeta+b_{\mu}
, while the
U(1)\otimes U(1)^{\text{ch}}
invariant quartic potential
(
5.9
) depends only upon
\sigma=|\Phi|
. In the presence of a non-zero
b^{\mu}
, the chiral chemical potential is
identified as the invariant combination
[
77
]

\mu_{5}^{2}=-(\partial^{\mu}\zeta+b^{\mu})(\partial_{\mu}\zeta+b_{\mu})=(\dot{\zeta}+b_{0})^{2}-(\nabla\zeta+\boldsymbol{b})^{2}

(6.9)

in the fluid description, analogous also to (
3.5
), and thus the effective potential to be minimized with respect to

\sigma
when
\mu_{5}\neq 0
is

\displaystyle V_{\text{eff}}(\sigma,\mu_{5})=-2\hskip 0.5pt\mu_{5}^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\sigma^{2}+V(\sigma)

(6.10)

showing that a non-zero chiral chemical potential enters
V_{\text{eff}}
with a negative sign, always tending
to destabilize the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetric state at
\sigma=0
. Indeed the condition

\displaystyle V_{\text{eff}}^{\prime}({\bar{\sigma}},\mu_{5})=-4\hskip 0.5pt\mu_{5}^{2}\hskip 0.5pt{\bar{\sigma}}+V^{\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})=0

(6.11)

with
\lambda>0
admits the two possible solutions

(a)

{\bar{\sigma}}=0\,,\qquad{\rm if}\quad 4\hskip 0.5pt\mu_{5}^{2}<\kappa\,,\qquad U(1)^{\text{ch}}\ \rm symmetric\,,

(6.12a)

(b)

{\bar{\sigma}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{4\hskip 0.5pt\mu_{5}^{2}-\kappa}{\lambda}}\neq 0\,,\quad{\rm if}\quad 4\hskip 0.5pt\mu_{5}^{2}>\kappa\,,\quad U(1)^{\text{ch}}\ \rm SSB.

(6.12b)

In the SSB case
(b)
, (
6.8
) together with (
6.9
) informs us that

\frac{n_{5}}{\mu_{5}}=\frac{1}{\mu_{5}}\,\sqrt{-J^{\mu}_{5}J_{5\hskip 0.5pt\mu}}=4\hskip 0.5pt{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}

(6.13)

connecting the EFT fluid description
(iii)
in the equilibrium ground state with the scalar field description. By standard arguments
[
77
]
this SSB state defines a relativistic superfluid with the polar angle
field
\zeta
the Goldstone mode of
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
SSB that dominates the long distance/low energy
spectrum and can be identified with the axion. Equation (
6.13
) may be viewed as a definition of
n_{5}
, but the essential scale of SSB is of course
\bar{\sigma}
.

In the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetric case
(a)
the bosonic fields
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
are a gapped doublet with
equal mass squared
\kappa-4\hskip 0.5pt\mu_{5}^{2}>0
. Thus they play no role at large distances and can be dropped
entirely in the low energy EFT, while with
g{\bar{\sigma}}=0
the fermions remain massless. In the limit of
non-self-interacting and massless fermions the axial anomaly must be taken into
account by adding the anomaly effective action (
3.1
) or (
3.2
) to the classical action of
(
6.1
). In other words in
(a)
, taking the quantum fermion loop and the axial anomaly into
account means making the replacement of the classical (
2.1
) coupled to electromagnetism by the
1PI effective action

\mathscr{L}^{\text{\ref{casea}}}_{\text{eff}}=-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}$}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pt\gamma^{\mu}\big(i\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}+eA_{\mu}\big)\psi+\big(\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}\big)\hskip 0.5ptJ_{5}^{\mu}+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}

(6.14)

in which the
\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\psi
term of (
2.1
) is included in (
3.2
) and no fermion self-interactions
appear explicitly. Since the anomaly leads to the fermion pairing as in Fig.
2
, single
fermonic excitations do not appear in the spectrum at zero temperature and the fermionic term in (
6.14
)
can also be dropped, so that we obtain the low energy effective action in the ASB case

\mathcal{S}_{\text{ASB}}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\left[-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}$}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+\big(\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}\big)\hskip 0.5ptJ_{5}^{\mu}+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\right]=\mathcal{S}_{\text{EM}}[A]+\mathcal{S}_{\rm anom}[\eta;A,b]

(6.15)

in the
T=0
vacuum, with
\mathcal{S}_{\rm anom}
given by (
3.2
). This is the low energy effective action
that applies for a WSM for Weyl nodes displaced in energy, when supplemented by the non-vacuum
-\varepsilon(n_{5})
,
as in Sec.
3
, or for a WSM placed in a strong magnetic field, with excitations aligned
with the
\boldsymbol{B}
field in the dimensional reduction of Sec.
4
. In both cases
\eta
describes
an axionic mode. The axion remains gapless if EM interactions can be treated as higher order, as in
Sec.
3
, or becomes gapped if they are of the same order, as in the effectively 2D case
of Sec.
4
.

We shall next demonstrate that the low energy effective action in the SSB case
(b)
leads to
essentially the same low energy effective action as (
6.15
), with the polar angle
\zeta

of the scalar
\Phi
field replacing the chiral angle and Clebsch potential
\eta
of
\mathcal{S}_{\rm anom}

in the ASB case
(a)
.

7
Perturbations from Equilibrium: Superfluidity and the Goldstone Sound Mode

In terms of the parameters of the potential
V(\sigma)
, the equilibrium values of the energy density and pressure are

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\kappa{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}+\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}$}\lambda{\bar{\sigma}}^{4}\,,

(7.1a)

\displaystyle\bar{P}

\displaystyle=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}$}\lambda{\bar{\sigma}}^{4}\,,

(7.1b)

where
{\bar{\sigma}}
is given by (
(b)
). The absence of any axial current in equilibrium requires
\boldsymbol{J}_{\!5}=0
so that

\displaystyle\nabla\bar{\zeta}=-\boldsymbol{b}\,,

(7.2)

while
\dot{\bar{\zeta}}=0
and
\bar{J}^{0}_{5}=\bar{n}_{5},\bar{\mu}_{5}^{2}=(b_{0})^{2}

in applying this EFT to WSMs in the case of Weyl nodes displaced in energy
and momentum as in Fig.
3
. From (
6.13
) and the minimization
condition (
6.11
) at
\sigma={\bar{\sigma}}
, we also have

\displaystyle 4\hskip 0.5pt{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}\bar{\mu}_{5}^{2}=\bar{\mu}_{5}\bar{n}_{5}={\bar{\sigma}}V^{\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})

(7.3)

in the equilibrium ground state of
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
SSB.

These equilibrium relations may also be used for small deviations away from equilibrium in the long wavelength limit of the EFT
in the effective boson description. Expressing the polar field variables as their equilibrium values plus small time and space
dependent perturbations,
i.e.

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle={\bar{\sigma}}+\delta\sigma\,,

(7.4a)

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\bar{\zeta}+\delta\zeta\,,

(7.4b)

\displaystyle\dot{\zeta}+b_{0}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mu}_{5}+\delta\dot{\zeta}\,,

(7.4c)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\zeta+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}(\delta\zeta)

(7.4d)

with
b_{0}=\mu_{5}
, and expanding the action (
6.6
) to the second order in perturbations around the SSB ground state,
we find (
cf.

[
77
]
)

\displaystyle S^{(2)}_{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\big[\delta\sigma(-\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,+M^{2}_{\sigma})\delta\sigma-4{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}\delta\zeta\,\raise 0.5pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hrule\hbox{\vrule height=7.22743pt\kern 7.22743pt\vrule}\hrule}$}\,\delta\zeta-16\bar{\mu}_{5}{\bar{\sigma}}\,\delta\sigma\,\delta\dot{\zeta}\big]\,,

(7.5)

where

\displaystyle M^{2}_{\sigma}=V^{\prime\prime}_{\text{eff}}({\bar{\sigma}})=V^{\prime\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})-4\bar{\mu}_{5}^{2}=V^{\prime\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})-\frac{V^{\prime}(\sigma)}{{\bar{\sigma}}}=2\lambda{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}=2\,\big(4\bar{\mu}_{5}^{2}-\kappa\big)\,.

(7.6)

The last term of (
7.5
) shows that there is a mixing between the gapless
Goldstone mode
\delta\zeta
and gapped
\delta\sigma
mode, which affects the low energy Goldstone
mode. Substituting the complex Fourier decomposition

\delta\zeta\sim e^{-i\omega t+i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}
we find the
2\times 2
Hermitian matrix

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\omega^{2}+\boldsymbol{k}^{2}+M^{2}_{\sigma}&4i\omega\bar{\mu}_{5}\\
-4i\omega\bar{\mu}_{5}&-\omega^{2}+\boldsymbol{k}^{2}\end{array}\right)

operating on the two-component vector
(\delta\sigma,2{\bar{\sigma}}\delta\zeta)
of perturbations. Setting the determinant of this matrix to zero gives
a quadratic equation for
\omega^{2}
, which yields the spectrum, consisting of one solution for
\omega^{2}
that is gapped at the scale

M^{2}_{\sigma}
, and a second solution at

\omega^{2}=v_{s}^{2}\,\boldsymbol{k}^{2}+{\cal O}\!\left(\!\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{k^{4}}{M^{2}_{\sigma}}$}\!\right)\,,

(7.10)

which is a gapless acoustic Goldstone mode with speed of sound that differs from the speed of ‘light’
c=1

of the Weyl node Fermi velocity. This sound speed is given instead by

\displaystyle v_{s}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{M^{2}_{\sigma}}{M^{2}_{\sigma}+16\bar{\mu}_{5}^{2}}=\frac{{\bar{\sigma}}\,V^{\prime\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})-V^{\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})}{{\bar{\sigma}}\,V^{\prime\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})+3V^{\prime}({\bar{\sigma}})}=\frac{\lambda{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}}{2\kappa+3\lambda{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}}=\frac{d\bar{P}/d{\bar{\sigma}}}{d\bar{\varepsilon}/d{\bar{\sigma}}}=\frac{d\bar{P}}{d\bar{\varepsilon}}\,,

(7.11)

which agrees with that obtained from a hydrodynamic approach to a sound mode.

Thus although
v_{s}\neq 1
due to the spontaneous breaking also of Lorentz symmetry by the condensate rest frame where
J^{0}_{5}=n_{5}
but
\boldsymbol{J}_{\!5}=0
, the Goldstone mode remains gapless. This is an axionic acoustic sound mode with speed (
7.11
). Note also that this agrees with the
v_{s}^{2}=1/3
and (
7.1
) agrees with the equation of state
p=\varepsilon/3
of free massless fermions if and only if
\kappa=0
, in which case the EFT is conformal. Any
\kappa\neq 0
corresponds to non-vanishing self-interactions of the fermions in the NJL description which breaks conformal invariance.

The eigenmode of (
7
) propagating with the sound speed
v_{s}
of (
7.11
) is the linear combination

\displaystyle\big[k^{2}(1-v_{s}^{2})+M_{\sigma}^{2}\big]\,\delta\sigma-8\bar{\mu}_{5}{\bar{\sigma}}\,\delta\dot{\zeta}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\rm or}\quad\qquad\delta\sigma\simeq\frac{8\bar{\mu}_{5}{\bar{\sigma}}}{M_{\sigma}^{2}}\,\delta\dot{\zeta}

(7.12)

to lowest order in
k^{2}/M^{2}_{\sigma}
for long wavelength acoustic excitations of the superfluid. Thus for
this linear combination the second action to second order of the perturbations (
7.5
) becomes

\displaystyle S^{(2)}_{\Phi}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{4{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}}{v_{s}^{2}}\right)\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\delta\zeta\left(\!-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}$}+v_{s}^{2}\,\nabla^{2}\right)\delta\zeta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\delta\zeta\left(\!-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}$}+v_{s}^{2}\,\nabla^{2}\right)\delta\zeta

(7.13)

for the Goldstone sound mode at long wavelengths. In obtaining this last relation (
3.11
) and
(
7.11
) has been used. Utilizing (
6.8
), (
7.4
) and (
7.12
), the linear
perturbations in the axial current are

\delta J^{0}_{5}=8{\bar{\sigma}}b_{0}\,\delta\sigma+4{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}\,\delta\dot{\zeta}=\frac{n_{5}}{\mu_{5}}\left(1+\frac{16\mu_{5}^{2}}{M_{\sigma}^{2}}\right)\delta\dot{\zeta}=\frac{n_{5}}{\mu_{5}}\frac{1}{v_{s}^{2}}\,\delta\dot{\zeta}=\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\,\delta\dot{\zeta}

(7.14)

and

\displaystyle\delta\boldsymbol{J}_{5}=-4{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}\nabla(\delta\zeta)=-\frac{\bar{n}_{5}}{\bar{\mu}_{5}}\nabla(\delta\zeta)\,.

(7.15)

Thus, the axial anomaly equation becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(\delta J^{\mu}_{5})=\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}-v_{s}^{2}\nabla^{2}\right)\delta\zeta=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi\,}\delta(\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{B})

(7.16)

if the anomaly source is also varied to linear order.

Comparing (
7.16
) for the perturbations in the phase field
\delta\zeta
in the case of SSB
in the effective potential
V_{\rm eff}
of (
6.10
) with the perturbations in the
Clebsch potential
\delta\eta
of the anomaly effective action (
3.12
), we see that they

coincide
, and with the same sound speed
v_{s}
when
\kappa=0
, corresponding to the
case of free fermions considered in Sec.
3
. This shows the case of ASB
and more familiar SSB in fact describe the same low energy hydrodynamic effective theory
of a chiral superfluid in the sense of
(iii)
of the Introduction. This result, perhaps
at first sight surprising, may be understood from the following considerations.

In the SSB case
(b)
the fermions acquire a mass gap
m=g{\bar{\sigma}}
from the Yukawa interaction in (
6.1
)
and no longer appear at distances greater than
1/m
. Thus they can be integrated out entirely at low
energies as in Appendix
Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass
. Since the EFT of (
6.1
) is renormalizable, the effect
of integrating out the fermions is to renormalize the parameters of the remaining bosonic effective theory,
and to add the finite anomaly action (
C.19
) in the bosonic sector. That the axial anomaly
survives intact even when the fermions become massive due to SSB is seen by the explicit computation of
the effective action, and in particular the term (
C.19
) of Appendix
Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass
,
which coincides with the last term of (
3.2
) with
\eta=\zeta
. Thus in this case
(b)
of SSB the
