effective bosonic action of the WSM becomes

\mathscr{L}_{\rm eff}^{\text{\ref{caseb}}}=-\tfrac{1}{4}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+\mathscr{L}_{\Phi}+\zeta{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\,,

(7.17)

with
\mathscr{L}_{\Phi}
given by (
6.6
). However in
\mathscr{L}_{\Phi}
there remain both the angular phase mode
\zeta
which
is gapless and the radial mode
\delta\sigma
with mass gap
M_{\sigma}
of (
7.5
)-(
7.6
).
The latter decouples at energy scales less than
M_{\sigma}
, so that the effective low energy action
for small fluctuations from equilibrium is only (
7.13
), but this is exactly the same
result following from the fluid effective action (
3.6
) in the ASB case for two Weyl nodes
displaced in energy, with
\zeta
taking the place of
\eta
. Thus in the SSB case
(b)
the
effective action for low energy gapless excitations only is

\mathcal{S}_{\text{SSB}}=\int d^{4}x\left[-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}$}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+\big(\partial_{\mu}\zeta+b_{\mu}\big)\hskip 0.5ptJ_{5}^{\mu}+\zeta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}-\varepsilon(n_{5})\right]

(7.18)

which is the same as (
6.15
) after the addition of the
-\varepsilon(n_{5})
term and replacement of

\eta
by
\zeta
. To lowest order in fluctuations from equilibrium the effective action in the SSB case
becomes

\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{\text{SSB}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{dn_{5}}{d\mu_{5}}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\delta\zeta\left(\!-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}$}+v_{s}^{2}\,\nabla^{2}\right)\delta\zeta+\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\delta\zeta{\mathscr{A}}_{4}

(7.19)

and its variation gives (
7.16
), with
v_{s}^{2}
given by (
7.11
).

The reason for identification of the Clebsch potential
\eta
with the bosonic polar phase angle
\zeta
of
the SSB phase is also not difficult to understand. Inspection of the Yukawa interaction in the polar representation

\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pt\Phi\hskip 0.5pt\psi=\hskip 0.5pt\sigma\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pt\exp\!\left(2i\zeta\gamma^{5}\right)\psi

(7.20)

shows that
\zeta
of the scalar
\Phi
can be shifted to the phase of the fermion fields by a
U(1)^{\text{ch}}

transformation (
2.3
) by (
5.4
), with
\beta=\zeta
. Then
e^{2i\zeta\gamma^{5}}
is the complex phase
factor of the fermion condensate matrix
\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle
. The fact that
\eta
appears in the EFT
of ASB in (
6.15
) in exactly the same way as
\zeta
does in the EFT of SSB in (
7.18
)
implies that there must be a fermion condensate in the ASB with this chiral phase, induced
by the axial anomaly alone, even it is not immediately apparent from the introduction of
\eta

in (
3.2
). As discussed further in Sec.
9
, the cases of ASB and SSB, which
appear at first sight to be quite different, are both associated with formation of a fermion chiral
condensate which spontaneously breaks the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry, and leads to the same low energy
collective axion excitation, although by a different route.

8
’t Hooft Anomaly Matching and Non-Decoupling of the Axial Anomaly

The effective action of the axial anomaly (
3.2
) is based on massless fermions,
m=0
.
As soon as the condition (
(b)
) for SSB is satisfied,
{\bar{\sigma}}\neq 0
, and the
fermion becomes massive, then for any finite fermion mass the fermionic contribution to the axial anomaly

{\cal A}
is reduced according to (
2.8
). This
m
dependent reduction in
{\cal A}
is the non-anomalous
contribution to the divergence of the chiral current
2im\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi
for free massive Dirac fermions,
according to (
A.10
), and turns off the anomaly completely in the
m\to\infty
limit, consistent with
decoupling of very heavy fermions in the Wilsonian effective action at low energies. This decoupling
occurs if the fermion mass is simply added to the free Dirac Lagrangian in (
2.1
),
explicitly breaking the
U(1)^{\rm ch}
invariance of the massless theory.

On the other hand if the fermion mass
m=g\bar{\sigma}
is a result of SSB in the EFT of Sec.
6
,
for which the axial current (
6.4
) is a classically conserved Noether current, the mass suppression
of the anomaly in (
A.10
) should be canceled by the bosonic contribution to the axial current (
6.8
).
That this is indeed the case can be shown from the classical equations of motion following from (
6.1
),
or in perturbation theory by computing the contribution to the axial anomaly of the bosonic term via its coupling
to the fermions (and hence electromagnetism) through the Yukawa interaction (
5.3
).
Expanding
-g\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\psi}\hskip 0.5pt\Phi\hskip 0.5pt\psi
to linear order in
\zeta
in the polar representation gives the
contribution to the bosonic part of the axial current in (
6.8
)

-4{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}(-ig{\bar{\sigma}})(2i)\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x^{\prime}\,\big\langle\partial^{\mu}\zeta(x)\zeta(x^{\prime})\big\rangle\,\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi(x^{\prime})=-8g{\bar{\sigma}}^{3}\partial_{x}^{\mu}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x^{\prime}\,\big\langle\zeta(x)\zeta(x^{\prime})\big\rangle\,{\cal P}(x^{\prime})

(8.1)

in position space, where
{\cal P}=\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi
. Using the definition of the canonically normalized Goldstone boson
propagator

4{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}\big\langle\zeta(x)\zeta(x^{\prime})\big\rangle=-i\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{e^{ik\cdot(x-x^{\prime})}}{k^{2}}

(8.2)

and going over to momentum space, the bosonic contribution to the amplitude (
A.1
) represented by
the diagram of Fig.
4
is

\Gamma_{5\,\Phi}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)=-2\hskip 0.5ptm\,\frac{k^{\mu}}{k^{2}}\,\Lambda_{5}^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)\,,

(8.3)

where
\Lambda_{5}^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)
is given by (
A.10
) and
m=g{\bar{\sigma}}
for the fermion mass has been used.
The contraction of (
8.3
) with
k_{\mu}
therefore gives
-2\hskip 0.5ptm\hskip 0.5pt\Lambda_{5}^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)
which is
equal and opposite in sign to the mass-dependent contribution of the fermion vertex
J_{5}^{\mu}[\psi]
given
by (
A.10
)
[
10
]
.

k
p
q
J^{\mu}_{5}[\Phi]
\zeta
J^{\alpha}
J^{\beta}

Fig.​ 4
:
The triangle diagram that contributes to the bosonic part of the axial current vertex
J^{\mu}_{5}[\Phi]
of (
6.8
) to two-photon amplitude
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
, given by (
8.1
) in position space or (
8.3
) in momentum space. Its contraction with
k_{\mu}
gives a contribution to the axial anomaly which
is equal and opposite to the mass-dependent contribution of the fermion vertex
J_{5}^{\mu}[\psi]
of (
6.7
)
given by (
A.10
).

Since the bosonic contribution to the non-anomalous divergence of the axial current (
6.3
) cancels the
fermionic mass contribution, the axial anomaly

\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}_{5}={\mathscr{A}}_{4}=\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\,\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F_{\mu\nu}=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\,\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}\,,

(8.4)

is completely unaffected by the onset of SSB and the fermion mass it generates. In fact this is a
necessary consequence of the Adler–Bardeen theorem which also holds in the
U(1)^{\rm EM}\otimes U(1)^{\rm ch}

sigma model of Sec.
6

[
58
]
.

Thus the fermions never decouple and continue to contribute the same amount (
8.4
) to the
axial anomaly which remains true
at any scale
in both the ASB case with nominally massless fermions and
the SSB case with the fermion mass generated by SSB of
U(1)^{\rm ch}
. This is an explicit demonstration
of ’t Hooft anomaly matching from UV to IR scales, and its renormalization group invariance. Even in the limit

m\to\infty
the full axial anomaly (
8.4
) remains unaltered and is simply transferred entirely
to the bosonic degrees of freedom, as shown by (
C.17
)-(
C.20
) in Appendix
Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass
.

9
The Goldstone Theorem for Anomalous Symmetry Breaking

The Noether current (
6.4
) of the classically
U(1)^{\rm EM}\otimes U(1)^{\rm ch}
invariant
effective action
S_{\text{cl}}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\mathscr{L}_{\text{cl}}
, given by (
6.1
) and
(
6.6
), satisfies the identity

\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}_{5}=i\,\frac{\!\delta S_{\text{cl}}}{\delta\psi(x)\,}\gamma^{5}\psi+i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\frac{\delta S_{\text{cl}}}{\delta\bar{\psi}(x)}+2\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}\frac{\delta S_{\text{cl}}}{\delta\phi_{1}(x)}-2\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{1}\frac{\delta S_{\text{cl}}}{\delta\phi_{2}(x)}\,,

(9.1)

so that the axial current is classically conserved on-shell by use of the equations of motion following from
variation of the action
S_{\text{cl}}
. In the quantum theory this relation becomes a WT identity

\left[\partial_{\mu}\frac{\delta}{\delta b_{\mu}(x)}-i\,\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi(x)}\,\gamma^{5}\psi-i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\frac{\delta}{\delta\bar{\psi}(x)}+2\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{1}\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi_{2}(x)}-2\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi_{1}(x)}\right]\Gamma={\mathscr{A}}_{4}\,,

(9.2)

for the 1PI effective action
\Gamma[A,b,\psi,\bar{\psi},\Phi]
defined by the Legendre transform (
1.2
),
(
1.3
), with the addition of the the anomalous axial current divergence

{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
of (
8.4
) at right. By the non-decoupling and ’t Hooft anomaly matching of
the previous section, the full anomaly (
8.4
) applies independently of the fermion mass
at all scales. If the fermions occur only in internal loops and no sources or mean fields are introduced
for them, the 1PI effective action
\Gamma[A,b,\Phi]
is independent of
\psi
and
\bar{\psi}
, and the corresponding
terms in the anomalous axial WT identity may be dropped.

In the familiar situation of SSB with no anomaly, the WT identity (
9.2
) with
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}=0
is the basis
upon which Goldstone’s theorem is established. In that case (
9.2
) is first integrated over the
spacetime volume
\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x
, selecting the Fourier component at zero four-momentum. Since the first
term on the left-hand side is a total divergence and the 1PI effective action
\Gamma
of (
1.2
) by
definition does not contain one-particle singularities at
p^{\mu}=0
, this total divergence term gives
zero contribution. Then the result is functionally differentiated once more with respect to
\phi_{2}(x^{\prime})
to obtain

\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\left[\phi_{1}(x)\,\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma}{\delta\phi_{2}(x)\delta\phi_{2}(x^{\prime})}\right]=\frac{\delta\Gamma}{\delta\phi_{1}(x^{\prime})}\,.

(9.3)

If this is evaluated at the extremum of
\Gamma
, at which the first variation at right vanishes, and if
this extremum occurs for a non-zero constant
\phi_{1}={\bar{\sigma}}
, we obtain

{\bar{\sigma}}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\left[\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma}{\delta\phi_{2}(x)\delta\phi_{2}(x^{\prime})}\right]=-{\bar{\sigma}}\,G^{-1}_{22}(p)\big|_{p=0}=0

(9.4)

in momentum space, since it follows from the definition of
\Gamma
in (
1.2
) that the second variation
of
\Gamma
at left is (minus) the inverse propagator
G_{22}^{-1}
of the
\phi_{2}
field. Since at zero four-momentum

G_{22}^{-1}(0)
is the
\phi_{2}
mass gap, (
9.4
) shows that the
\phi_{2}
scalar is a massless Goldstone boson
in the SSB state where
{\bar{\sigma}}\neq 0
.

Now we may ask how this standard result is affected by the presence of the anomaly
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
in the anomalous WT identity
(
9.2
). Note first that the anti-symmetric variation in (
9.2
) becomes

\left[2\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{1}\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi_{2}(x)}-2\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi_{1}(x)}\right]\Gamma=\frac{\delta\Gamma}{\delta\zeta(x)}

(9.5)

in the polar representation (
6.5
). Thus repeating the step above of integrating over the spacetime volume

\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x
, setting to zero the integral of the total divergence, but now functionally differentiating
with respect to the polar angle
\zeta(x^{\prime})
gives

\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma}{\delta\zeta(x)\delta\zeta(x^{\prime})}=\frac{\delta{\mathscr{A}}_{4}(x)}{\delta\zeta(x^{\prime})}=0\,.

(9.6)

Since the expression at left is proportional to the
\zeta
field inverse propagator at
p^{\mu}=0
, the anomalous
term
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
does not spoil Goldstone’s theorem or give rise to a mass gap of the polar
\zeta
mode,
provided that the anomaly
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}
itself is independent of
\zeta
, at least to first order at the extremum
of the effective action
\Gamma
.

For the scalar EFT of Sec.
6
, (
9.6
) in the polar representation is clearly equivalent
to the standard form of (
9.4
) by the change to polar field variables (
6.5
). However,
the results of Secs.
3
and
7
, in particular comparison of (
3.12
)
and (
7.16
) shows that they are identical also upon substitution of
\eta
for
\zeta
. One can check that
this general theorem holds in the EFT of Secs.
3
and
7
,
with
\zeta
is replaced by
\eta
in the former case. It fails in the 2D dimensional reduction of
Sec.
4
because the 2D anomaly itself depends on the chiral rotation angle
\eta=\zeta

at linear order, by (
4.5
) and (
4.12
), so that the right side of (
9.6
) is
non-vanishing after dimensional reduction. This dependence generates an effective mass gap for the
would-be Goldstone mode for dynamical electric fields by the Anderson–Higgs mechanism.

Note that (
9.4
) and (
9.6
) hold and the
\zeta
angular mode is gapless even if Lorentz
invariance is broken and the
p^{2}
terms in
G_{22}^{-1}(p^{0},\boldsymbol{p})
or
G_{\zeta}^{-1}(p^{0},\boldsymbol{p})
of (
9.6
)
have unequal coefficients and the velocity of propagation differs from
c
(or
v_{F}
). This is clearly
relevant to the case of Weyl nodes displaced in energy considered in Secs.
3
and

7
, where a preferred rest frame is defined by the non-zero
\mu_{5}
. In this case the
speed of propagation of the Goldstone mode is not
v_{F}
(the surrogate for the relativistic speed of
light in a WSM), but the sound speed (
3.15
) or (
7.11
) instead, determined by the equilibrium
equation of state and
dp/d\varepsilon
. Although the CDW propagates at a different sound speed, it remains gapless,
and the Goldstone theorem (
9.6
) holds in the case of ASB as well as the more familiar SSB case,
resolving the apparent paradox of ASB giving rise to a Goldstone mode despite the anomaly explicitly
violating the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry. It also supports by explicit examples in WSMs the more abstract
argument that a gapless Goldstone mode should be expected also in the case of anomalous continuous symmetries,
based on the construction of non-invertible defect boundary operators
[
78
,
79
]
.

The alert reader will have noticed that (
9.4
) is a relation for a canonically normalized
scalar field
\phi_{2}
and is multiplied by the expectation value
{\bar{\sigma}}
in the SSB case. In the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}

symmetric phase (
(a)
)
{\bar{\sigma}}=0
and (
9.4
) becomes null: there is no Goldstone boson.
On the other hand (
9.6
) is expressed entirely in terms of the polar phase angle field
\zeta
, which is
dimensionless, and not a canonically normalized field (except in
d=2
). Referring to the explicit
examples of Secs.
3
or
7
, we see that the missing factor with the correct
dimensions is supplied by
dn_{5}/d\mu_{5}
in the case of Weyl nodes displaced in energy. Thus this quantity
proportional to
{\bar{\sigma}}^{2}
must be non-vanishing for (
9.6
), or the corresponding relation
in terms of
\eta
, and the
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry must be spontaneously broken for (
9.6
) to
be non-null and a Goldstone boson to exist.

That
{\bar{\sigma}}
must be non-zero for a Goldstone boson to exist leads to the conclusion that a fermion condensate

\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle\neq 0
must be present also in the ASB case of Weyl nodes separated by energy, at non-zero

\mu_{5}
, even though it does not appear explicitly in (
3.12
) and (
3.13
). This is also
indicated in the bosonic description by the fact that any non-zero chiral chemical potential with even free
massless fermions in Sec.
3
, corresponding to the critical value of
\kappa=0
in
V(\sigma)
,
destabilizes the symmetric ground state to the broken symmetry state. This is the chiral analog of recent
studies of non-zero chemical potentials and charge densities being connected with SSB of
U(1)
symmetry
and superfluid behavior as well
[
80
]
.

Since no direct four-fermion self-interaction was postulated in the ASB case for a system of apparently
‘free’ fermions, one may ask what the mechanism of symmetry breaking is that can produce a superfluid state
and Goldstone mode. Since the fermion pairing in the ASB case is due to the axial anomaly itself, one
must recognize that the Weyl fermions are not actually ‘free,’ but still subject to the electromagnetic
interaction that leads to the anomaly. We are thus led to the conclusion that it is the same electromagnetic
interactions of the fermions responsible for the axial anomaly that are also responsible for the formation
of non-zero
\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle\neq 0
condensate, spontaneously breaking the
U(1)^{\rm ch}
symmetry in addition.

In fact it has been known for some time that long range magnetic interactions which are not Debye screened in
a plasma of finite charge density and chemical potential
\mu
lead to logarithmic divergences of the fermion
self-energy
\Sigma\sim\ln(E-\mu)
for excitations
E
close to the Fermi
surface
[
81
,
82
]
, an effect which is enhanced in degenerate relativistic
plasmas
[
83
]
, and which leads to non-Fermi liquid behavior of the plasma
[
84
]
.
The corresponding calculations have not been performed for a degenerate chiral plasma of massless fermions
at non-zero
\mu_{5}
to our knowledge. It is natural to conjecture however that the same unscreened long range
magnetic interactions provide an attractive channel for fermion pair interactions of Fig.
5
,
which generates an effective four-fermion interaction even if none were present initially. In that case
one would also expect that evaluation of the fermion self-energy in Fig.
6
in the chiral
plasma will lead to a non-trivial solution to the fermion gap equation with a chiral condensate

\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle\neq 0
that spontaneously breaks
U(1)^{\text{ch}}
symmetry in the case of non-zero

\mu_{5}
as well.

\bar{\psi}
\psi
\bar{\psi}
\psi

Fig.​ 5
:
The attractive channel of magnetic interactions of fermions leading to Cooper pairing.
The photon line must be dressed by interactions in the chiral plasma if Debye screening is present
at non-zero
\mu_{5}
.

\bar{\psi}
\psi

Fig.​ 6
:
The one-loop fermion self-energy
\Sigma
to be calculated for non-zero chiral potential
\mu_{5}
needed for
the gap equation in the ASB case. The photon propagator is the same as in Fig.
5
.

If that is indeed the case, the superfluid axion CDW mode in the ASB case would be accounted for as the bound
state of the Cooper pairs produced by the anomaly itself as in Fig.
2
, purely by their
electromagnetic interactions for arbitrarily small coupling
e
, consistent with the existence of the axial
anomaly due to those same interactions with electromagnetism. This same calculation at finite temperature
T

and
\mu_{5}
should also determine the critical temperature
T_{c}\sim\mu_{5}
at which the condensate first vanishes
and with it the Goldstone mode and superfluid behavior. The fermion anomaly diagram of Fig.
1

itself should also be calculated at non-zero
\mu_{5}
to directly verify the existence of the gapless pole
with the CDW sound speed
v_{s}^{2}=\frac{1}{3}v_{F}^{2}
of (
3.15
), and complete the picture in the ASB case.

10
Summary and Discussion

Exploiting the formal identity of gapless Weyl nodes in WSMs to massless Weyl fermions in relativistic QFT,
we have proposed in this paper a UV renormalizable effective theory in Sec.
6
that
encompasses both the case of SSB by four-fermion interactions of the NJL kind as in Sec.
5
, and
the case when these interactions between the Weyl nodes are weak and below the SSB threshold of

G\leqslant G_{c}
or
\kappa\leqslant 0
. This provides a consistent QFT framework and basis for deriving controlled
low energy EFT limits for WSMs in both cases. There is of course no claim that (
6.1
) represents
the ‘true’ microscopic degrees of freedom of a WSM, which depend upon the electronic valence and conduction
bands of the material. The point is rather that by expressing the EFT in terms of a small number
of finite parameters that are insensitive to short distance physics, which can be determined
by measurements in each WSM, the Landau paradigm of EFTs of types
(i)
and
(iii)
can be
found for WSMs, and their consequences reliably deduced independently of the microscopic physics.
Integrating out the Weyl fermions when they become gapped can also be studied, as in
EFTs of type
(ii)
in Appendix
Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass
.

That the axial anomaly is a consequence purely of the symmetries of the ground state, independently of
high energy or short distance physics has been emphasized in Appendix
Appendix A. The Chiral Anomaly in QED
4
and its IR Aspect
, to elucidate
why it plays an important role in the low energy EFT of a WSM. The massless
1/k^{2}
pole and finite
sum rule associated with the anomaly are low energy features of the axial anomaly, which leads to a propagating
collective axion mode that should be present in WSMs even for cases in which direct fermion-fermion
interactions are weak or absent. In the framework proposed by starting with (
6.1
),
the axial anomaly and the axionic collective mode to which it leads can be derived from first principles of QFT
in WSMs, which directly gives a superfluid EFT of type
(iii)
. This axion excitation is
a collective mode of correlated fermion pairs at the Fermi surface, and arises in a zero temperature
WSM, whether or not there are strong four-fermion interactions of the Weyl modes. In the case
of strong four-fermion interactions the axion is a Goldstone mode of the SSB of
U(1)^{\rm ch}

symmetry breaking with a non-zero scalar order parameter
{\bar{\sigma}}
in its ground state.

Eq. (
9.6
) shows that despite the axial anomaly explicitly contributing an anomalous term to the

U(1)^{\rm ch}
WT identity in (
9.2
), it does not spoil Goldstone’s theorem as long as
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}

itself is independent of
U(1)^{\rm ch}
rotations to first order, when evaluated at the ground state
extremum of the effective action
\Gamma
. Thus the effective action of the axial anomaly itself
(
3.2
) predicts the existence of a propagating gapless CDW which is linearly coupled to the

\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}
anomaly, which can be identified as an axion even in WSMs with weakly interacting
Weyl modes, where such a collective boson mode might not have been expected. This extends Goldstone’s theorem
to the ASB case, supporting more formal arguments in
[
78
,
79
]
with an
example of laboratory realizable system of anomalous symmetry breaking.

In both the SSB and ASB cases the axion is a collective Goldstone excitation of a low
temperature superfluid phase of a WSM, as in both cases the low energy EFT is of the
fluid form
(iii)
of a relativistic superfluid with the Fermi velocity
v_{F}
of the
Weyl nodes replacing the speed of light
c
of relativistic QFT. In both cases the low-energy
EFT of axions
\eta=\zeta
in WSMs takes the form

S_{\rm WSM}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\left[-\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}$}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+\big(\partial_{\mu}\eta+b_{\mu}\big)J^{\mu}_{5}-\varepsilon(n_{5})+\eta\hskip 0.5pt{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\right]

(10.1)

where
b^{\mu}=(b^{0},b^{i})
is the axial vector potential, whose equilibrium value is one-half the separation of
Weyl nodes in energy and spatial momentum respectively,
cf.
Fig.
3
, and
{\mathscr{A}}_{4}

is the axial anomaly (
2.10
) which is independent of scale. In the case of Weyl nodes displaced
from each other in energy,
\mu_{5}\neq 0
,
cf.
(
6.13
), the gapless axionic excitation from
equilibrium following from (
10.1
) is described by the quadratic action (
7.19
) and the linear
equation of motion (
7.16
) with
\delta\zeta=\eta
, together with Maxwell’s equations following from
(
10.1
). For a WSM placed in a strong enough magnetic field such that the fermions are all in their
lowest Landau level, (
10.1
) reduces instead to the two-dimensional effective action (
4.4
)
or (
4.7
), for excitations along the
\boldsymbol{B}
direction. This dimensionally reduced effective
action is equivalent to massless QED
2
,
i.e.
the Schwinger model
[
62
]
, in which
case the axionic excitation acquires the squared mass gap
M^{2}=2\alpha eB/\pi
, in analogy with the
Anderson–Higgs mechanism in a superconductor.

The
\eta
variable was introduced in (
3.2
) as a Clebsch potential in an effective fluid
description, with no apparent scalar order parameter of spontaneous
U(1)^{\rm ch}
symmetry breaking
to which it is attached. Yet the fact that the axionic mode in this case obeys exactly the same
equation of motion (
3.12
) as the phase mode
\zeta
(
7.16
) of the bosonic order parameter
is a clear indication that the two cases of ASB and SSB are in fact very closely related.
In particular a chiral condensate
\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle
scalar order parameter that spontaneously
breaks the
U(1)^{\rm ch}
symmetry (even with the anomaly present) should exist for which

\exp(2i\eta)
is its complex phase. Verifying this will require finding a non-trivial solution
of the gap equation resulting from the self-energy of Fig.
6
with
\langle\bar{\psi}\psi\rangle\neq 0
.
In that case the spontaneous breaking of the classical
U(1)^{\rm ch}
symmetry would be due
to fermion pairing through the attractive channel of Fig.
5
and the axion mode
of Fig.
2
the result of the same electromagnetic interactions that give rise
to the axial anomaly itself. This same self-energy at finite temperature
T
should determine when
the coherent state Cooper pairing dissociates and the superfluid to normal Fermi liquid fluid phase
transition takes place. This would be expected to be at temperatures
T\sim\mu_{5}=(\mu_{5}/10{\text{ meV}})\,116
K.

The WSM realization of axionic excitations opens new opportunities for experimental tests of the theoretical ideas presented in this paper. For example, the frequency dependence of the AC current in (
3.8
) induced in external electromagnetic fields with non-zero
\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}
may be used to experimentally probe the propagating axionic mode. The axionic mode and resulting current response may also be induced by other sources, e.g. external strain fields. We will discuss these and other potential experimental signatures in a forthcoming publication.

The EFT approach to WSMs presented here provides an interesting application of the ’t Hooft anomaly matching condition
across scales from the UV renormalizable (
6.1
)-(
6.2
) to the macroscopic superfluid effective
theory of emergent axion excitations of (
6.15
) or (
7.18
). An intriguing possibility is that
additional insights into the operation of the axial anomaly, anomaly matching and possible emergent axionic
mode in the Standard Model of particle physics could result from detailed study of the properties and
excitations of WSMs, to which the EFT of this paper can be applied.

Appendix A. The Chiral Anomaly in QED
4
and its IR Aspect

In this appendix we briefly review the evaluation of the fermion triangle amplitude in Fig.
1
,

\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)=i\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}y\,e^{ip\cdot x+iq\cdot y}\ \langle 0|{\cal T}J_{5}^{\mu}(0)J^{\alpha}(x)J^{\beta}(y)|0\rangle\Big|_{A=0}

(A.1)

in momentum space by imposing the appropriate symmetries of the low energy theory.
Conventions used in this and subsequent appendices for Dirac
\gamma
-matrices satisfying the anti-commutation property
\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=-2\,\eta^{\mu\nu}
with
\eta_{\mu\nu}={\rm diag}(-1,1,1,1)
, are such that
\gamma^{0}=(\gamma^{0})^{\dagger}
is Hermitian while the
\gamma^{i}=-(\gamma^{i})^{\dagger}
are anti-Hermitian.
The chirality matrix
\gamma^{5}\equiv i\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma^{\beta}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}/4!=i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}=(\gamma^{5})^{\dagger}

(with
\epsilon_{0123}=1
) is also Hermitian, with eigenvalues
\pm 1
corresponding to right-handed or
left-handed chiralities respectively.

First, observe that a possible linear UV divergence of
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
proportional to an
arbitrary four-vector, if present, would imply that the vacuum or ground state
is not Lorentz invariant. Hence the first requirement
(a)
of Lorentz invariance automatically forces this possible linear divergence to zero identically. Explicitly, since

\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
must be a function only of the two independent four-momenta
p
and
q
,
and with three Lorentz indices must transform as a third-rank tensor, it follows
that
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
must be expressible as a sum of all possible third-rank
tensors
\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
, each of which can depend only upon the
two independent external
4
-momenta
p^{\mu}
and
q^{\nu}
. If the vacuum ground state is also invariant under the discrete symmetry
of spatial reflection parity (P) combined with time reversal (T), then each of the
\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
basis tensors must also involve one factor
the totally antisymmetric Levi–Civita pseudo-tensor
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}
, since
J^{\mu}_{5}
and hence
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}
is odd under P and T separately, but invariant (even) under the combined operation of PT.

Next, electric current conservation at the two electromagnetic current vertices
J^{\alpha}
and
J^{\beta}
requires that each of the basis tensors
\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
into which (
A.1
) can be expanded must satisfy

p_{\alpha}\,\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)=0=q_{\beta}\,\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}\,,\qquad{\rm for\ each}\ i\,.

(A.2)

Defining the psuedo-tensor
\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)
by (
2.7
), which satisfies

p_{\alpha}\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)=0=q_{\beta}\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)

(A.3)

it is easily shown by exhaustive enumeration that there are exactly six tensors meeting all of the symmetry
requirements of
\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
, which are listed in Table
1

[
37
]
.

\begin{array}[]{|@{\hspace{.8cm}}c @{\hspace{.8cm}}|@{\hspace{.8cm}}c @{\hspace{.8cm}}|}\hline\cr\hskip 22.76228pt\lx@intercol\hfil i\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\vrule\hskip 22.76228pt&\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\hskip 22.76228pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\vrule\hskip 22.76228pt&-p\cdot q\,\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}p_{\gamma}-p^{\beta}\,\upsilon^{\mu\alpha}(p,q)\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\\
\hline\cr\hskip 22.76228pt\lx@intercol\hfil 2\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\vrule\hskip 22.76228pt&p^{2}\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}q_{\gamma}\,+p^{\alpha}\upsilon^{\mu\beta}(p,q)\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\\
\hline\cr\hskip 22.76228pt\lx@intercol\hfil 3\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\vrule\hskip 22.76228pt&p^{\mu}\,\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\hskip 22.76228pt\lx@intercol\hfil 4\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\vrule\hskip 22.76228pt&p\cdot q\,\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}q_{\gamma}+q^{\alpha}\,\upsilon^{\mu\beta}(p,q)\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\\
\hline\cr\hskip 22.76228pt\lx@intercol\hfil 5\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\vrule\hskip 22.76228pt&-q^{2}\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}p_{\gamma}\,-q^{\beta}\upsilon^{\mu\alpha}(p,q)\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\\
\hline\cr\hskip 22.76228pt\lx@intercol\hfil 6\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\vrule\hskip 22.76228pt&q^{\mu}\,\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 1
:
The
6
third-rank Lorentz pseudo-tensors linear in
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}
satisfying (
A.2
),
only 4 of which are linearly independent due to the Schouten identity
[
37
,
85
]
.

Thus, general conservation and invariance principles prescribe that the triangle amplitude (
A.1
) must be expressible as

\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)=\sum_{i=1}^{6}f_{i}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})\,\tau_{i}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)\,,

(A.4)

where the form factors
f_{i}=f_{i}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2})
are Lorentz scalar functions of the three independent
Lorentz invariants
p^{2},q^{2}
and
k^{2}=(p+q)^{2}
. Since each of the six basis tensors satisfying (
A.2
)
in Table
1
are homogeneous of degree
3
in the external momenta,
3
powers of momenta have been
extracted from the triangle amplitude
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
. Hence the remaining scalar amplitude functions
f_{i}
are of degree
-2
therefore may be expressed
as Feynman integrals that are quadratically
convergent
,
i.e.
independent of any high energy
or short distance cutoff. The result is that requirements (
(a)
) and (
(b)
)
render the triangle amplitude
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
UV finite and well-defined.

The full amplitude (
A.4
) must also be Bose symmetric,

\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)=\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\beta\alpha}(q,p)

(A.5)

under simultaneous interchange of
p,q
and
\alpha,\beta
. Owing to the Bose symmetry and overcompleteness of the six tensors in Table
1
, finally only
two of the scalar coefficient functions,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
say, need to be independently computed.
The finite scalar coefficient functions are given in the literature
[
86
,
9
]
,
and most conveniently expressed in terms of integrals

f_{i}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})=\frac{e^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \frac{c_{i}(x,y)}{D}

(A.6)

over the Feynman parameters
(x,y)
with
0\leqslant x+y\leqslant 1
, where the
c_{i}(x,y)
are the simple polynomials

\displaystyle c_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle=c_{4}(x,y)=xy\,,

(A.7)

\displaystyle c_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=c_{5}(y,x)=x(1-x)

\displaystyle c_{3}(x,y)

\displaystyle=c_{6}(x,y)=0\,,

and the denominator
D
of (
A.6
) is

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=p^{2}x(1-x)+q^{2}y(1-y)+2p\cdot q\,xy+m^{2}

\displaystyle=(p^{2}\,x+q^{2}\,y)(1-x-y)+xy\,k^{2}+m^{2}\equiv xy\big(k^{2}+S\big)\,,

(A.8)

which defines the quantity
S(x,y;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})
for arbitrary finite fermion mass
m
. Both
D
and
S

are strictly positive for spacelike momenta,
k^{2},p^{2},q^{2}>0
.

Computing the contraction with the momentum
k_{\mu}=(p+q)_{\mu}
entering at the axial vector vertex results in the
well-defined finite result (
2.6
) with

\displaystyle{{\cal A}}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})

\displaystyle=2p\cdot q\,f_{1}+p^{2}f_{2}+q^{2}f_{5}=\frac{e^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \frac{D-m^{2}}{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(1-2m^{2}\,\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\,\frac{1}{D}\right)

(A.9)

by (
A.6
), (
A.8
) and Table
1
, which is (
2.8
) of Sec.
2
.
The second term proportional to
m^{2}
is what would be expected from the axial vector divergence
\partial_{\mu}J_{5}^{\mu}=2im\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi
,
cf.
(
2.5
), following from use of the on-shell Dirac equation for fermions
of mass
m
,
i.e.

2im\!\int\!d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\!\int\!d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}y\,e^{ip\cdot x+iq\cdot y}\big\langle{\cal P}(0)J^{\alpha}(x)J^{\beta}(y)\big\rangle\Big|_{A=0}\!\equiv 2\hskip 0.5ptm\hskip 0.5pt\Lambda_{5}^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)=-\frac{e^{2}m^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\,\upsilon^{\alpha\beta}(p,q)\!\int_{0}^{1}\!dx\!\int_{0}^{1-x}\!\!dy\,\frac{1}{D}

(A.10)

in the same Feynman parameter representation, with
{\cal P}=\bar{\psi}\gamma^{5}\psi
the pseudoscalar density
[
57
]
.

If the fermion mass
m=0
, this last term of (
A.9
) proportional to
m^{2}
vanishes, and (
2.9
) is the
finite and non-zero axial current anomaly. On the other hand, in the limit
m^{2}\to\infty
, the denominator
D

of (
A.8
) can be replaced by
m^{2}
, and the entire
{\cal A}
of (
A.9
) is canceled since

2\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy=1
, hence
{\cal A}=0
, so that the fermion decouples entirely and
there is no anomaly pole in this limit.

One should recognize that conservation at the axial vector vertex
J_{5}^{\mu}
could be required, by defining

\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}(p,q)
to violate the charge conservation condition
(b)
instead. The anomaly is
fundamentally a conflict between symmetries, forcing a choice between the classical conservation
equations (
2.5
), which can only be decided by additional physical input. Since charge conservation
is well-established, and it is known that the
m\to 0
limit is not equivalent to the
m=0
theory
when
e\neq 0
, which limit is beset with
infrared
(IR) divergences,
cf.

[
87
,
88
,
89
]

and Appendix
Appendix A. The Chiral Anomaly in QED
4
and its IR Aspect
, the choice is made to preserve
U(1)^{\text{EM}}
and discard
U(1)^{\text{ch}}

as a fundamental symmetry.

This derivation of the chiral anomaly and anomaly pole relies only upon the low energy symmetries of the theory. Thus exactly the same anomaly is obtained by any method
which satisfies these same symmetry requirements, among them Pauli–Villars regularization
[
90
]
, dimensional
regularization
[
56
]
, heat kernel methods
[
91
]
, or Fujikawa’s regularization of the fermion
functional integral for the axial current
[
72
,
73
]
. The fact that these various methods were
developed in QFT to regularize its short distance behavior, as the first step to removing UV divergences in a renormalization
procedure, led to some obscuring of the physical basis of the axial anomaly as an essentially IR phenomenon.
The only role these more sophisticated regularization methods play is simply to require
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}
to satisfy conditions

(a)
-
(d)
, which are properties of the vacuum or ground state of the theory.

To emphasize its independence of extreme high energy or short distance physics, and relation to fermion pairing,
one may also derive (
2.6
), (
2.8
) by a dispersion approach. For this one observes that the absorptive
part of the triangle amplitude (
A.4
) is determined by the on-shell matrix elements
\sum_{n}\langle 0|J_{5}^{\mu}|n\rangle\langle n|J^{\alpha}J^{\beta}|0\rangle
for timelike
k_{\mu}
, with
|n\rangle
a complete set of two-particle intermediate fermion/anti-fermion
states. This on-shell absorptive part is well-defined and finite, and in no need of regularization. Then the full amplitude
is obtained from the Kramers–Kronig dispersion relation

\displaystyle f_{i}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{k^{2}+s}\ \rho_{i}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})\,,

(A.11a)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\big(f_{i}(k^{2}\!-\!i\epsilon;\,p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})\big)=\pi\rho_{i}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})\Big|_{s=-k^{2}}

(A.11b)

in the complex
k^{2}
plane. Inserting
1=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\ \delta(s-S)
into (
A.6
), and using
D=xy(k^{2}+S)

from (
A.8
) allows us to express these absorptive parts by

\rho_{i}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})=\frac{e^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \frac{c_{i}(x,y)}{xy}\ \delta\big(s-S(x,y;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})\big)

(A.12)

in the Feynman parameter integral representation. Using (
A.7
) it follows from (
A.12
) that the combination
of absorptive parts appearing in (
2.8
) or (
A.9
)

2p\cdot q\,\rho_{1}+p^{2}\rho_{2}+q^{2}\rho_{5}=(k^{2}+s)\,\rho_{1}-\frac{e^{2}\,m^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\!dx\int_{0}^{1-x}\!\frac{dy}{xy}\ \delta\big(s-S(x,y;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})\big)

(A.13)

which vanishes at
s=-k^{2}
when
m=0
. Thus there is no anomaly in the absorptive part of (
A.4
).

On the other hand from
c_{1}(x,y)=xy
given in (
A.7
), we have from (
A.12
) that

\rho_{1}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})=\frac{e^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \delta\!\left(\!s-\frac{(p^{2}x+q^{2}y)(1-x-y)+m^{2}}{xy}\right)\neq 0\,,

(A.14)

so that using this in (
A.9
) with (
A.13
), the
s
integral is trivially
performed by definition of the Dirac
\delta
-function, and one obtains

\displaystyle{{\cal A}}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{k^{2}+s}\,\Big(2p\cdot q\,\rho_{1}+p^{2}\rho_{2}+q^{2}\rho_{5}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\rho_{1}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})-\frac{e^{2}\,m^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\ \frac{\,1}{D}

(A.15)

Comparing (
A.15
) to (
A.9
) we have finally that

\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\rho_{1}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\rho_{1}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2}\!=\!0)={{\cal A}}(k^{2};p^{2},q^{2};m^{2}\!=\!0)=\frac{e^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}

(A.16)

independently of
m
(and also of
p^{2},q^{2}\geqslant 0
), which can be verified directly from (
A.14
).

Thus the axial anomaly in the real or dispersive part of the triangle amplitude at
m=0
, (
2.9
) is associated
with the
UV finite
spectral sum rule (
A.16
) which holds
for all

p^{2},q^{2},m^{2}>0
, and also
in the limiting case where
p^{2}=q^{2}=0
and
m^{2}\to 0^{+}
. This is possible despite the fact that
\rho_{1}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})

vanishes pointwise as
p^{2},q^{2},m^{2}\to 0^{+}
(in any order) for any non-zero
s
, because

\lim_{p^{2},q^{2}m^{2}\to 0^{+}}\rho_{1}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2})=\frac{e^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\,\delta(s)

(A.17)

becomes a singular Dirac
\delta
-function in this limit, as may also be verified by inspection of (
A.14
).

The imaginary, absorptive part of the axial current divergence
{\cal A}
of (
2.6
) or (
A.13
)
vanishes for
m=0
, in accordance with expectations from the classical theory, since
(k^{2}+s)\rho_{1}(s)\big|_{s=-k^{2}}=0
.
However computing the real, dispersive part of
k_{\mu}\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}
requires dividing (
A.13
) by
k^{2}+s

(the relativistic analog of the energy denominator of first order perturbation theory) which cancels the first factor,
leaving
\rho_{1}(s)
itself. By direct calculation the spectral function
\rho_{1}(s)
does not vanish, even in the
m\to 0
limit.
The reason for this can be traced to the fact that even in the massless limit, a charged Dirac fermion can make a transition
to a virtual state of the opposite helicity by the emission of a photon
[
87
,
88
,
89
]
, that is to say,
massless Dirac fermions do
not
become two decoupled Weyl fermions in the presence of electromagnetism. This
results in the matrix elements
\langle n|J^{\alpha}J^{\beta}|0\rangle
of the two electromagnetic current vertices to the fermion
pair intermediate state in the imaginary part of the triangle diagram Fig.
1
remaining non-vanishing
in the massless fermion limit, and contributing positively to
\rho_{1}(s;p^{2},q^{2};m^{2}\!=\!0)
as in (
A.14
).
That its integral over
s
, as in (
A.16
), gives a finite constant result independent of
p^{2},q^{2}\geqslant 0

is a statement of the finite constant residue of the
1/k^{2}
anomaly pole for
m=0
in the longitudinal part
of the triangle amplitude, protected by the Adler–Bardeen theorem
[
58
]
.

It is significant that this dispersive analysis, relying only upon the well-defined, finite imaginary
absorptive parts of the triangle amplitude
\Gamma_{5}^{\mu\alpha\beta}
, matrix elements of low energy states,
and
unsubtracted
Kramers–Kronig dispersion relations (
A.11
), leads to the full amplitude
obeying conditions
(a)
-
(d)
of Sec.
2
, and the axial anomaly (
2.10
),
without encountering UV divergent integrals requiring regularization at any step. The appearance of the

1/k^{2}
gapless pole singularity with residue fixed by the finite sum rule (
A.16
) demonstrate
that the axial anomaly is an infrared (more properly, relativistic lightcone) feature of the low energy effective
theory, independent of UV short distance physics
[
88
,
92
,
37
]
.

Appendix B. The Axial Anomaly for WSMs in a Constant Uniform Magnetic Field

In a constant uniform magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}\!=B\bf\hat{x}
, the Fourier transform of the
electromagnetic potential
\tilde{A}_{\gamma=2,3}(q)
is non-zero in the transverse
\bf\hat{y}
,

\!\bf\hat{z}
directions, while the constancy of
B=F_{23}
implies that

q_{2}\tilde{A}_{3}(q)-q_{3}\tilde{A}_{2}(q)=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}z\,e^{-iq\cdot z}(q_{2}A_{3}(z)-q_{3}A_{2}(z))=-iB\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta^{\hskip 0.5pt4}(q)

(B.1)

proportional to a
\delta
-function at
q^{\mu}=0
. The vectors
k^{\mu}=p^{\mu}+q^{\mu}=p^{\mu}
of Fig.
1
are then equal to each other. If their spatial components are taken to lie also in the
\bf\hat{x}
direction, parallel to
\boldsymbol{B}
, then the
\tau_{2}
tensor in Table
1
is the only one contributing to
\tilde{\Gamma}_{5}^{\mu\alpha\gamma}(p,q)\tilde{A}_{\gamma}(q)
, which for
\gamma=2,3
is non-zero only for
\mu=a,\alpha=b
ranging over the
0,1
components. We then obtain

\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\Gamma_{5}^{\,ab\gamma}(p,q)A_{\gamma}(q)\Big|_{\boldsymbol{k}_{\perp}=\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}=0}=iBf_{2}(k^{2};k^{2},0)\big(k^{2}\epsilon^{ab}-k^{b}k^{c}\hskip 0.5pt\epsilon^{a}{\!}_{c}\big)

(B.2)

for the constant, uniform
\boldsymbol{B}
field, in terms of the anti-symmetric tensor
\epsilon^{ab}\equiv\epsilon^{ab23}
of the remaining two
a,b=0,1
spacetime indices. Next noting from (
A.8
) that
D=p^{2}x(1-x)=k^{2}x(1-x)
for
m=0
and
q=0
, the function
f_{2}
evaluates simply to

f_{2}(k^{2};k^{2},0)\Big|_{m=0}=2f_{1}(k^{2};k^{2},0)\Big|_{m=0}=\frac{e^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\,\frac{1}{k^{2}}=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\,\frac{1}{k^{2}}\,,

(B.3)

explicitly showing the
1/k^{2}
pole in this case. Thus the full anomaly vertex
\Gamma_{5}^{\,ab\gamma}(p,q)

is given entirely by its longitudinal projection in this case of constant, uniform
\boldsymbol{B}
.

In fact this result for the (three-point) triangle amplitude is closely related to the
(two-point) polarization tensor

\Pi_{2}^{ab}(k)=i\langle j^{a}j^{b}\rangle=\frac{1}{\pi k^{2}}(k^{a}k^{b}-k^{2}\eta^{ab})

(B.4)

of massless fermions in
1\!+\!1
dimensional QED
2
. This 2D polarization tensor (
B.4
) satisfies
the usual vector WT identity
k_{a}\hskip 0.5pt\Pi_{2}^{ab}(k)=0
of electric current conservation. On the other hand
the 2D chiral current is

\tilde{\jmath}^{\,a}=-\epsilon^{a}{\!}_{\hskip 0.5ptb}\,j^{b}\,,

(B.5)

which is dual to the electric current
j^{b}
.
Thus the chiral current polarization

\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{ab}(k)\equiv i\langle\tilde{\jmath}^{\hskip 0.5pta}j^{b}\rangle=-\epsilon^{a}{\!}_{c}\Pi_{2}^{cb}(k)=\frac{1}{\pi k^{2}}(k^{2}\epsilon^{ab}-k^{b}k^{c}\epsilon^{a}{\!}_{c})

(B.6)

satisfies the anomalous WT identity

k_{a}\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{ab}(k)=-\frac{k_{a}\epsilon^{ab}}{\pi}

(B.7)

which is the axial anomaly

\partial^{a}\tilde{\jmath}_{a}={\mathscr{A}}_{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\hskip 0.5pt\epsilon^{ab}F_{ab}=\frac{1}{\pi}\hskip 0.5ptE

(B.8)

in
1+1
dimensions, illustrated in Fig.
7
. Here
E=F_{10}
the electric field strength in
the
\bf\hat{x}
direction parallel to
\boldsymbol{B}
.

k
k
\tilde{\jmath}^{\hskip 0.5pta}
\!\!j^{b}

Fig.​ 7
:
The one-loop axial anomaly in two dimensions.

The dimensional reduction to
1+1
dimensions results from the fact that with (
B.3
) and (
B.6
)
(
B.2
) may be written as

\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\Gamma_{5}^{ab\gamma}(p,q)A_{\gamma}(q)\Big|_{\boldsymbol{k}_{\perp}=\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}=0}=2i\alpha B\,\hskip 0.5pt\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{(5)\,ab}(k)\,,

(B.9)

which is non-zero only for
\mu=a,\alpha=b=0,1
in a constant uninform magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}=B\bf\hat{x}
in the
indicated limits. Thus the three-point axial triangle anomaly for massless fermions in
3+1
dimensions becomes proportional
to the two-point polarization tensor
\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{ab}
the 4D axial anomaly becomes proportional
to the 2D axial anomaly,
i.e.

{\mathscr{A}}_{4}\big|_{B}=\frac{eB}{2\pi}{\mathscr{A}}_{2}=\frac{2\alpha EB}{\pi}\to 2\alpha B\,\big(E+\partial_{a}b^{a}\big)\,,

(B.10)

after rescaling the electric field
E\to eE
parallel to
\boldsymbol{B}
, in order to accord with standard (Heaviside–Lorentz) conventions in 4D electromagnetism. In the last replacement of (
B.10
) we have allowed
for the fact that in 2D the chiral potential
b_{a}=\epsilon_{a}{\!}^{c}A_{c}
is also dual to the electric potential,

i.e.

b_{0}=A_{1},b_{1}=A_{0}
, and
\epsilon^{ab}\partial_{a}A_{b}\to\epsilon^{ab}A_{b}+b^{a}
, so that
{\mathscr{A}}_{2}
is given by (
B.8
) when both the electric and axial potentials are non-vanishing.

A consequence of this dimensional reduction from 4D to 2D is that the gapless collective boson composed of fermion/anti-fermion pairs propagating along the
\boldsymbol{B}
direction, described by the
1/k^{2}
anomaly pole in 4D, becomes in this case for
{\bf k}_{\perp}=0
, precisely the
1/k^{2}
propagator pole of the effective boson of the 2D Schwinger model for vanishing 2D electric coupling constant
[
62
,
70
,
36
]
.

The dimensional reduction (
B.9
) from 4D to 2D for semimetals in a constant, uniform magnetic field
amounts to the LLL approximation for the
3+1
dimensional axial polarization tensor

i\big\langle{\cal T}J_{5}^{a}(t,x,{\bf y})J^{b}(t,x^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})\big\rangle_{{}_{\!\!LLL}}=2i\alpha B\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\int\frac{dk}{2\pi}\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}{\bf k}_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{ik\cdot(x-x^{\prime})+i{\bf k}_{\perp}\cdot({\bf y}-{\bf y}^{\prime})}e^{-k_{\perp}^{2}/2eB}\hskip 0.5pt\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{ab}(\omega,k)

(B.11)

in terms of the
1+1
dimensional one (
B.4
), along the magnetic field direction
[
93
,
94
,
95
,
96
,
97
]
. Integrating over the transverse
{\bf y}
direction, the exponential factor in (
B.11
) is set to unity, so that the Fourier transform in
t,x
gives

\displaystyle i\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}x\,e^{-ik\cdot(x-x^{\prime})}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt2}{\bf y}\,\big\langle{\cal T}J_{5}^{a}(t,x,{\bf y})J^{b}(t,x^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})\big\rangle_{{}_{\text{LLL}}}

\displaystyle=2i\alpha B\,\widetilde{\Pi}_{2}^{ab}(k)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\Gamma_{5}^{ab\gamma}(p,q)A_{\gamma}(q)\Big|_{\boldsymbol{k}_{\perp}=\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}=0}

(B.12)

by (
B.9
). Thus the axial anomaly of the 4D triangle diagram coincides with the 2D chiral polarization of gapless fermions in a constant, uniform magnetic field in the LLL approximation. The 4D axial current along the magnetic field direction
J_{5}^{a}=(eB/2\pi)j_{5}^{a},a=0,1
in terms of the 2D chiral current
j_{5}^{a}
since
eB/2\pi
is just the electron number density per unit area in the LLL.

The relation (
B.12
) may also be understood as an expression of the low energy or infrared nature of the axial anomaly. Since the higher Landau levels are effectively gapped by
eB
, only the LLL’s gapless excitations contribute to the excitations parallel to the
\boldsymbol{B}
direction at distance scales much larger than
1/\sqrt{eB}
. If an external electric field is turned on adiabatically, only fermions in the LLL can be excited, and the
d=4
axial anomaly factorizes into its
d=2
counterpart with a transverse density proportional to the uniform magnetic field strength
B

[
6
,
98
]
. The contribution of the axial anomaly to the effective action of massless fermions becomes exact in this limit. Thus the 4D triangle anomaly provides a relevant interaction in the low energy, long distance EFT of gapless fermionic modes in Weyl and Dirac semimetals placed in a constant, uniform
\boldsymbol{B}
field.

Appendix C. Integrating Out the Fermions in the Limit of Large Fermion Mass

Here we will present a derivation of the effective action (of type
(ii)
) in a WSM from first principles. The action we consider is given by (
2.1
) and (
5.1
). The four-fermion interaction, which is of the NJL type, is the same as the one considered in the condensed-matter literature, e.g.
[
14
]
. Its treatment in the high-energy physics literature is highly-developed due to the interest in the NJL model as a model for QCD mesons; see e.g.
[
99
,
100
,
101
]
. Our computations will use the covariant derivative expansion techniques described in
[
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
,
109
]
. We will focus only on specific terms of interest and not attempt to derive the full effective field theory.

After the introduction of Hubbard–Stratonovich fields
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
given by (
5.6
), the action becomes that of (
2.1
) with zero bare mass along with (
5.5
). The fermions can now be integrated out to obtain the effective action of
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},A_{\mu}
and
b_{\mu}
only:

S_{\text{eff}}=-i\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt{\rm Tr}\ln[\gamma^{\mu}(i\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{\mu}+eA_{\mu}+b_{\mu}\gamma^{5})-g\phi_{1}-ig\phi_{2}\gamma^{5}]-\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\frac{g^{2}}{G}(\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2})\,,

(C.1)

where
{\rm Tr}
denotes the full trace over coordinate space and matrices. With standard manipulations assuming continuous momentum fermionic states to express
{\rm Tr}
, this can be brought to the form

S_{\text{eff}}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\bigg\{i\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\textrm{tr}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n}\bigg(\frac{i\not{D}+\not{b}\gamma^{5}-g\phi_{1}-ig\phi_{2}\gamma^{5}}{\not{k}-m}\bigg)^{n}-\frac{1}{G}\big[(g\phi_{1}+m)^{2}+g^{2}\phi_{2}^{2}\big]\bigg\}\,,

(C.2)

where
\not{D}=\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}
,
D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}-ieA_{\mu}
, tr
denotes the trace over matrices only, and we shifted
g\phi_{1}\to g\phi_{1}+m
. Below we will perform explicit evaluations of terms of interest in
S_{\text{eff}}
diagramatically. Our calculations will capture terms of the effective action expanded in fields and derivatives. Results for more terms than we consider here are included in
[
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
]
.

First of all, for
g\neq 0
the extremization condition
\delta S_{\text{eff}}/\delta\phi_{1}|_{\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=0,A_{\mu}=b_{\mu}=0}=0
gives

\frac{2m}{G}=i\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\textrm{tr}\frac{1}{\not{k}-m}=4\hskip 0.5ptmI_{1}\,,

(C.3)

where
1
1

1

To evaluate the integral we Wick rotate by
k^{0}=ik_{E}^{0}
. With our metric conventions,
k^{2}=k_{E}^{2}
.

I_{1}=-i\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{k^{2}+m^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Lambda\gg m}}{{=}}\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\Big(\Lambda^{2}-m^{2}\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\Big)\,.

(C.4)

We use a hard cutoff
\Lambda
to regularize the loop integral
I_{1}
. We will be working with
\Lambda\gg m
.
2
2

2

As usual, the cutoff drops out of all physical quantities because
G
depends on
\Lambda
. For example, if
m
is measured, then the gap equation (
C.3
) simply gives us the dependence of the bare coupling
G
on
\Lambda
, with
m
fixed at the measured value. Observables depend on the measured
m
and not the cutoff-dependent
G
.
With the definition
G_{c}=8\pi^{2}/\Lambda^{2}
, (
C.3
) becomes

2m\Big(\hskip 0.5pt\frac{1}{G}-\frac{1}{G_{c}}\hskip 0.5pt\Big)=-\frac{m^{3}}{4\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\,.

(C.5)

If
G<G_{c}
, then the only solution of (
C.5
) is
m=0
, but if
G>G_{c}
we have a solution with
m\neq 0
which signals spontaneous breaking of chiral symmetry. To examine stability of the
m=0
vacuum we evaluate

-\!\left.\frac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{eff}}}{\delta\phi_{1}^{2}}\right|_{m=\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=0,A_{\mu}=b_{\mu}=0}=\frac{2g^{2}}{G}-ig^{2}\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\textrm{tr}\frac{1}{\not{k}^{2}}=2g^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{G}-\frac{1}{G_{c}}\Big)\,.

(C.6)

Thus, for
G<G_{c}
(resp.
G>G_{c}
) the
m=0
vacuum is stable (resp. unstable). In the remainder of this section we work in the broken phase (
m\neq 0
) and focus on terms in the effective action involving
\phi_{2}
,
A_{\mu}
and
b_{\mu}
.

Quadratic terms in
\phi_{2}
with no derivatives are generated by

\mathscr{L}_{\phi_{2}^{2}}=\hbox to63.07pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.31047pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(C.7)

and a kinetic term for
\phi_{2}
is generated from the sum of diagrams

\mathscr{L}_{D^{2}\phi_{2}^{2}}=\hbox to59.97pt{\vbox to46.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.98232pt\lower-22.92537pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-26.6493pt}{-17.59236pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.92 -13.92 M 15.3 -13.92 C 15.3 -13.15 14.68 -12.54 13.92 -12.54 C 13.15 -12.54 12.54 -13.15 12.54 -13.92 C 12.54 -14.68 13.15 -15.3 13.92 -15.3 C 14.68 -15.3 15.3 -14.68 15.3 -13.92 Z M 13.92 -13.92}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.59236pt}{-16.59236pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=g^{2}I_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}^{2}D^{2}-2g^{2}J_{2}^{\mu\nu}\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}^{2}D_{\mu}D_{\nu}\,,

(C.9)

where

I_{2}=-i\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{(k^{2}+m^{2})^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Lambda\gg m}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\Big(1-\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\Big)\,,

(C.10)

J_{2}^{\mu\nu}=-i\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{(k^{2}+m^{2})^{3}}\,.

(C.11)

With a gauge invariant regulator or the introduction of suitable counterterms we may obtain
J_{2}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\mu\nu}I_{2}
. Then,

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{D^{2}\phi_{2}^{2}}=-g^{2}I_{2}(2\hskip 0.5ptD^{\mu}\phi_{2}D_{\mu}\phi_{2}-2\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}^{2}D^{2})=-g^{2}I_{2}\hskip 0.5pt[D^{\mu},\phi_{2}][D_{\mu},\phi_{2}]\,.\end{split}

(C.12)

In our case,
[D_{\mu},\phi_{2}]=\partial_{\mu}\phi_{2}
. As a result, in the effective action we will have the terms

S_{\phi_{2}^{2}}+S_{\partial^{2}\phi_{2}^{2}}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,(\mathscr{L}_{\phi_{2}^{2}}+\mathscr{L}_{\partial^{2}\phi_{2}^{2}})=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\big[\!-\!g^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{G}-2\hskip 0.5ptI_{1}\big)\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}^{2}-g^{2}I_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial^{\mu}\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{\mu}\phi_{2}\big]\,.

(C.13)

The
\phi_{2}^{2}
term is eliminated by the gap equation (
C.3
) and we are left with

S_{\partial^{2}\phi_{2}^{2}}=-\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,g^{2}I_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial^{\mu}\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{\mu}\phi_{2}\,,

(C.14)

which is the kinetic term for the massless Nambu–Goldstone boson of spontaneous chiral symmetry breaking.

There are no terms linear in
\phi_{2}
and
b
with no derivatives because the corresponding trace is zero, but with one derivative in addition we have the sum of diagrams

\mathscr{L}_{D\phi_{2}b}=\hbox to59.57pt{\vbox to52.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.53685pt\lower-26.18645pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(C.15)

The first three diagrams are equal, as are the last three. A straightforward calculation gives

S_{\partial\phi_{2}b}=-\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,4g\hskip 0.5ptmI_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial^{\mu}\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5ptb_{\mu}\,.

(C.16)

Further terms of relevance to our discussion arise at fifth order. More specifically, we are interested in terms with one power of
\phi_{2}
and four derivatives:

\mathscr{L}_{D^{4}\phi_{2}}=\hbox to68.58pt{\vbox to53.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.14934pt\lower-27.396pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{13.04243pt}{11.83926pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!+\cdots=-\frac{4g}{m}I_{3}\hskip 0.5pt\epsilon^{\kappa\lambda\mu\nu}\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5ptD_{\kappa}D_{\lambda}D_{\mu}D_{\nu}=-\frac{g}{m}I_{3}\hskip 0.5pt\epsilon^{\kappa\lambda\mu\nu}\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5pt[D_{\kappa},D_{\lambda}][D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]\,,

(C.17)

where

I_{3}=-im^{2}\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{(k^{2}+m^{2})^{3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Lambda\gg m}}{{=}}\frac{1}{32\pi^{2}}\,.

(C.18)

Since
[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]=ieF_{\mu\nu}
, we find

S_{\phi_{2}\partial^{2}A^{2}}=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\frac{2g\hskip 0.5pte^{2}}{m}I_{3}\hskip 0.5pt\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5ptF_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{g\hskip 0.5pte^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\frac{\phi_{2}}{m}F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(C.19)

Collecting the contributions (
C.14
), (
C.16
) and (
C.19
), we have

S_{\text{eff}}\supset\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\Big(\!-\!g^{2}I_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial^{\mu}\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial_{\mu}\phi_{2}-4g\hskip 0.5ptmI_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\partial^{\mu}\phi_{2}\hskip 0.5ptb_{\mu}+\frac{g\hskip 0.5pte^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{\phi_{2}}{m}F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\Big)\,.

(C.20)

Further terms involving derivatives of
b_{\mu}
should be included, e.g. an
F^{5}_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{5\hskip 0.5pt\mu\nu}
term with
F^{5}_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}b_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}b_{\mu}
(see
[
108
]
), but we omit them here.

Terms quadratic in
A
and terms quadratic in
b
are given respectively
by

\mathscr{L}_{D^{2}}=\hbox to74.3pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.92296pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.38889pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 M 39.37 0 C 39.37 10.87 30.56 19.69 19.69 19.69 C 8.81 19.69 0 10.87 0 0 C 0 -10.87 8.81 -19.69 19.69 -19.69 C 30.56 -19.69 39.37 -10.87 39.37 0 Z M 19.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-19.58995pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 0 M 40.75 0 C 40.75 0.76 40.13 1.38 39.37 1.38 C 38.61 1.38 37.99 0.76 37.99 0 C 37.99 -0.76 38.61 -1.38 39.37 -1.38 C 40.13 -1.38 40.75 -0.76 40.75 0 Z M 39.37 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.98576pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-2\hskip 0.5ptI_{1}\hskip 0.5ptD^{2}+4\hskip 0.5ptJ_{1}^{\mu\nu}D_{\mu}D_{\nu}\,,

(C.21)

where

J_{1}^{\mu\nu}=-i\int\frac{d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{(k^{2}+m^{2})^{2}}\,,

(C.22)

and

\mathscr{L}_{b^{2}}=\hbox to65.77pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.65767pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.38889pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 M 39.37 0 C 39.37 10.87 30.56 19.69 19.69 19.69 C 8.81 19.69 0 10.87 0 0 C 0 -10.87 8.81 -19.69 19.69 -19.69 C 30.56 -19.69 39.37 -10.87 39.37 0 Z M 19.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-15.32466pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 0 M 40.75 0 C 40.75 0.76 40.13 1.38 39.37 1.38 C 38.61 1.38 37.99 0.76 37.99 0 C 37.99 -0.76 38.61 -1.38 39.37 -1.38 C 40.13 -1.38 40.75 -0.76 40.75 0 Z M 39.37 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.98576pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-(4\hskip 0.5ptm^{2}I_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\eta^{\mu\nu}+2\hskip 0.5ptI_{1}\hskip 0.5pt\eta^{\mu\nu}-4\hskip 0.5ptJ_{1}^{\mu\nu})\hskip 0.5ptb_{\mu}b_{\nu}\,.

(C.23)

To preserve gauge invariance we need to demand
\mathscr{L}_{D^{2}}=0
, or
J_{1}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}I_{1}
. With this choice we still get a mass for the axial vector.
Adding this to (
C.20
) we get

S_{\text{eff}}\supset\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\Big(\!-\!4\hskip 0.5ptm^{2}I_{2}\hskip 0.5ptB^{\mu}B_{\mu}+\frac{g\hskip 0.5pte^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{\phi_{2}}{m}F_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\Big)\,,\qquad B_{\mu}=b_{\mu}+\frac{g}{2\hskip 0.5ptm}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{2}\,.

(C.24)

An infinitesimal chiral rotation of the original fermion with
x
-dependent parameter
\beta=\beta(x)
, namely

\psi\to(1+i\beta\gamma^{5})\psi\,,\qquad\bar{\psi}\to\bar{\psi}(1+i\beta\gamma^{5})\,,

(C.25)

which results in the appearance of an anomaly term
\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\frac{e^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\beta\hskip 0.5ptF_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
in the effective action, leaves the theory (
C.24
) invariant if
g\phi_{2}\to g\phi_{2}-2\hskip 0.5ptm\hskip 0.5pt\beta
and
b_{\mu}\to b_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\beta
. This guarantees that (
C.17
) is the only source of the anomaly in this picture.

A quadratic and a quartic contribution in
\phi_{1}
are generated via

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.59236pt}{-17.5368pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-\frac{g^{4}}{16\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\,\phi_{1}^{4}\,.

(C.28)

Thus,

\mathscr{L}_{\phi_{1}^{2}}+\mathscr{L}_{\phi_{1}^{4}}=-\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\Big(\frac{2}{G}-\frac{2}{G_{c}}\Big)\phi_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\frac{g^{4}}{4\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\,\phi_{1}^{4}\,,

(C.29)

which is consistent with (
C.6
). Requiring that

m^{2}=-\kappa/\lambda,\kappa<0
, now gives us the gap equation (
C.5
) once again. Comparing with (
5.9
), we find (
5.12
) and (
5.13
).

Note that the quartic in
\phi_{2}
contribution is

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.59236pt}{-17.5368pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-g^{4}I_{2}\,\phi_{2}^{4}\,,

(C.30)

which is exactly as required for consistency with (
5.9
), since
\sigma^{2}=\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2}
.

With the polar representation (
6.5
),
\phi_{1}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2}=\sigma^{2}
and the field
\zeta
is dimensionless. Using (
6.5
) in our action, given by (
2.1
) with zero bare mass along with (
5.5
), we get

S=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\Big\{\bar{\psi}\big[\gamma^{\mu}(i\hskip 0.5pt\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}+eA_{\mu}+b_{\mu}\gamma^{5})-g\hskip 0.5pt\sigma e^{2i\zeta\gamma^{5}}\big]\psi-\frac{g^{2}}{G}\hskip 0.5pt\sigma^{2}\Big\}\,.

(C.31)

To compute the effective action associated with integrating out
\psi
we define a chirally rotated fermion
\psi^{\prime}=e^{i\zeta\gamma^{5}}\psi
. Then,

S=\int d^{\hskip 0.5pt4}x\,\Big\{\bar{\psi}^{\prime}[\gamma^{\mu}(i\hskip 0.5pt\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{${}^{\leftrightarrow}$}\hskip-9.0pt\partial_{\mu}+eA_{\mu}+B\smash{{}^{\prime}}{\!\!}_{\mu}\gamma^{5})-g\hskip 0.5pt\sigma]\psi^{\prime}-\frac{g^{2}}{G}\hskip 0.5pt\sigma^{2}+\frac{e^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\hskip 0.5pt\zeta\hskip 0.5ptF_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\Big\}\,,\qquad B\smash{{}^{\prime}}{\!\!}_{\mu}=b_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\zeta\,,

(C.32)

and the fermion
\psi^{\prime}
can again be integrated out as above. It is important to note that the chiral rotation of the fermion results in a contribution to the effective action associated with the transformation of the path-integral measure, namely the last term in (
C.32
). This contribution has been discussed in
[
109
]
using covariant derivative expansion methods. The linear dependence on
\zeta
in (
C.32
) follows from the Adler–Bardeen theorem
[
58
]
, together with Bardeen’s general results for the axial anomaly
[
110
]
.
